Title: Towards discrete convex analysis over classical root systems

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.16090v1

Published Time: Tue, 18 Aug 2026 01:35:41 GMT

Markdown Content:
# Towards discrete convex analysis over classical root systems 

Yuni Iwamasa ∗

August 18, 2026 

Abstract 

Discrete Convex Analysis (DCA) is a discrete analog of continuous convex analysis, orig-inally proposed as a unified theoretical framework for efficiently solvable combinatorial op-timization problems. Recently, DCA has proven to be a powerful tool across diverse fields, ranging from operations research to economics and pure mathematics. Motivated by the broad applicability of DCA, this paper establishes a unified theory of discrete convex analysis over discrete structures arising from classical root systems, extending the usual setting of the integer lattice, which essentially corresponds to type A. We adopt the vertex set of the Euclidean Coxeter complex as the primal discrete domain for L-convexity, and the root lattice as the dual discrete domain for M-convexity. Using the associated polyhe-dral structures, we formulate L- and M-convex functions together with notions of integrality determined by the root system. We show that local optimality guarantees global optimality for these functions. Furthermore, we establish that integral L-convex functions and inte-gral M-convex functions correspond one-to-one via the discrete Fenchel–Legendre conjugate, thereby extending the conjugacy in the original DCA from type A to all classical root systems. 

Keywords: Discrete convex analysis, L-convexity, M-convexity, Fenchel–Legendre conjugate, root systems, spherical Coxeter complexes, Euclidean Coxeter complexes, root lattices, submodular functions, matroids.

# 1 Introduction 

Discrete Convex Analysis (DCA) , initiated by Murota [47, 48], is a theory that combines convex analysis with submodular and matroid theory. DCA originally emerged as a theoretical frame-work for efficiently solvable combinatorial optimization problems related to network flows and submodular/matroid optimization. Recently, however, it has appeared not only in the field of combinatorial optimization but also in various applied mathematical fields such as economics, game theory [52], and operations research [62, 15, 17, 16], as well as in pure mathematics such as algebraic geometry (e.g., Lorentzian polynomials [9]); see also [50, 54] for recent surveys. DCA consists of two classes of convex functions defined on the integer lattice, called L-convex functions and M-convex functions . The former is a generalization of submodular functions [22] and is defined by the so-called discrete midpoint convexity , and the latter is a generalization of (valuated) matroids [51, 57] and is defined based on the simultaneous exchange property of matroids. For these functions, local optimality guarantees global optimality. Furthermore, an 2intriguing fact is that the integer-valued L- and M-convex functions (referred to as the integral 

L- and M-convex functions) correspond one-to-one via the discrete Fenchel–Legendre conjugate .The purpose of this research is to establish a unified theory of DCA over discrete structures arising from classical root systems . Specifically, in this paper, for classical root systems, we introduce the concepts of L-convexity over the vertex set of the Euclidean Coxeter complex and M-convexity over the root lattice . Furthermore, by formulating an appropriate notion of integrality for each root system, we show that integral L-convex functions and integral M-convex functions correspond one-to-one via the discrete Fenchel–Legendre conjugate. Here, a root system (see e.g., [37, 38]) is a finite set consisting of (dual) vectors, called roots ,of a Euclidean space that possesses an integral structure capturing the symmetries of finite re-flection groups (see Section 3.1 for the formal definition). It was originally introduced in the late 19th century to classify complex semisimple Lie algebras, and now serves as a fundamental mathematical structure across various disciplines, including representation theory [37, 27], alge-braic geometry [63], mathematical physics [13], and combinatorics [5]. Irreducible root systems are completely classified. The classification contains four infinite families, called the classical 

root systems, of types A, B, C, and D. Numerous extensions of L- and M-convexity, including those of submodular functions and matroids, have been proposed so far, and most of them closely relate to classical root systems. The original L- and M-convexity can be viewed as those over discrete structures arising from the root system of type A. As variants in terms of type B, ∆ -matroids [7, 20, 14] (see also [6, Chapter 4]) and BS-convex sets/functions [23] (see also [39]) generalize matroids and M-convex sets/functions, respectively. Bisubmodular functions [58, 8] are a generalization of submodular functions in terms of type B or C (see also [2]), and UJ-convex functions [23] are a generalization of L-convex functions in terms of type C. Even ∆-matroids , which form a special subclass of ∆-matroids, can be viewed as a generalization of matroids in terms of type D (see [6, Chapter 4]). Furthermore, jump systems [8] were formulated by extending the exchange axioms of ∆-matroids to the integer lattice. Valuated (even) ∆-matroids [21, 66, 65] and M-convex functions on jump systems [49, 53] are valuated generalizations of the corresponding discrete structures, which are defined based on the simultaneous exchange property. In combinatorial optimization, these serve as mathematical structures capturing polynomial-time solvable combinatorial optimization problems that are not covered by standard DCA, including non-bipartite matchings and related problems; see also, e.g., [19, 49, 43, 42, 41, 53, 40] and references therein. Related generalizations to arbitrary finite reflection groups include Coxeter matroids [6] and, more recently, Coxeter submodular functions [2]. In addition, L-convex functions particularly admit generalizations as discretizations of convex functions defined on metric spaces more general than Euclidean spaces, specifically CAT(0) spaces (see [10]). These include k-submodular functions [36], L♮-convex functions on rooted trees [44], and L-convex functions on buildings [30, 31, 28, 33], which can also be interpreted as convex functions defined over discrete structures arising from root systems, particularly, Euclidean buildings [1]. The discrete version of convex analysis capturing the extended concepts associated with other root systems has not been well established. Indeed, in the fields of discrete mathematics and combinatorics, although generalized discrete structures have been extensively investigated, these mathematical objects have rarely been analyzed from the viewpoint of convex analysis. Meanwhile, in the context of combinatorial optimization, the underlying discrete structures for the theory have conventionally been restricted to the integer lattice, although the integer lattice might not necessarily be the appropriate discrete structure for developing convex analysis for other types. Furthermore, quantitative extensions such as valuated ∆-matroids and M-convex functions on jump systems in [21, 66, 65, 49, 53] are defined based on the simultaneous exchange property, which enables us to easily guarantee that local optimality implies global optimality. 3However, this introduces a gap with the underlying combinatorial structures, i.e., ∆-matroids and jump systems, which do not always satisfy this property (see [12]). Consequently, in the context of combinatorial optimization, these valuated concepts are essentially restricted to functions defined on a special subclass of ∆-matroids or jump systems where the simultaneous exchange property holds. This structural discrepancy between the general combinatorial structures and the restricted domains of their valuated counterparts has acted as a major barrier to constructing a theory that treats both the combinatorial structures and the valuated functions in a unified manner. To establish such a unified framework, one must return to (continuous) convex analysis (see e.g., [60]). In continuous optimization, convexity ensures that local optimality implies global optimality and establishes a general duality framework known as Fenchel duality . A crucial role in Fenchel duality is played by the fact that proper closed convex functions on a Euclidean space 

V and those on its dual space V ∗ correspond one-to-one via the Fenchel–Legendre conjugate .DCA successfully discretizes this conjugacy. It is worth noting that discretizing the theory of convex analysis is far from trivial. The continuous notion of convexity depends on continuous operations, such as the midpoint of two points, which generally fall outside a discrete domain; there is no immediately canonical way to define convexity on discrete structures. Furthermore, preserving the conjugacy requires carefully designing discrete structures both in the primal and dual spaces so that these are compatible with the Fenchel–Legendre conjugate. Discrete structures arising from root systems—the Euclidean Coxeter complex and the root lattice —provide a suitable framework for developing the discrete Fenchel–Legendre conjugate. Recall that continuous conjugacy connects a primal space V and its dual space V ∗ and that an element in V ∗ acts as a linear function on V , geometrically representing the normal vector of a hyperplane. To construct consistent discrete domains in both spaces, one can introduce a highly regular, locally finite arrangement of affine hyperplanes in V by a root system. This arrangement partitions V into a simplicial complex known as the Euclidean Coxeter complex , whose vertex set serves as the primal discrete domain. Correspondingly, the normal vectors of these hyperplanes, which are the roots themselves, generate a lattice in V ∗ called the root lattice . By adopting the root lattice as the dual discrete domain, the geometric and algebraic correspondence between the hyperplanes in V and their normal vectors in V ∗ is maintained. This structural relationship provides the discrete primal-dual framework necessary to preserve conjugacy. The main contribution of this paper is to establish DCA over discrete structures arising from classical root systems. Specifically, the details are summarized as follows: 

• As a geometric foundation for the theory in Euclidean spaces, we define L- and M-convex cones and polyhedra and L- and M-sublinear functions using the structure of root systems, together with appropriate notions of integrality . Integral L-convex polyhedra correspond to the geometric realizations of convex subcomplexes of the Euclidean Coxeter complex, whereas the integrality of M-convex polyhedra is defined with respect to the root lattice. The integral variants of L- and M-sublinear functions are defined so that the correspon-dences between polyhedra and sublinear functions preserve integrality. 

• We then formulate locally polyhedral L- and M-convex functions in Euclidean spaces. They are defined by the condition that their minimizer sets under linear perturbations are L-or M-convex polyhedra, or equivalently, that their directional derivatives are L- or M-sublinear functions. Such a function is called primal-integral if its minimizer sets are integral, dual-integral if its directional derivatives are integral, and integral if it satisfies both conditions. It is worth mentioning that the integrality is defined relative to the root system and need not coincide with integer-valuedness outside type A. Defining L- and M-convexity in terms of minimizer sets is similar to the definitions of BS-convex functions [23] 4and valuated ∆-matroids in the sense of [18], whose underlying ∆-matroids need not satisfy the simultaneous exchange property. It therefore allows M-convex functions to be defined without imposing the simultaneous exchange property. 

• We define L-convex sets/functions on the vertex set of the Euclidean Coxeter complex 

and M-convex sets/functions on the root lattice , which constitute the discrete L- and M-convexity studied in this paper. An L-convex set is the vertex set of a convex subcomplex of the Euclidean Coxeter complex, whereas an M-convex set is the set of lattice points of an integral M-convex polyhedron. The corresponding discrete functions are defined as the restrictions of primal-integral locally polyhedral L- and M-convex functions to the re-spective discrete domains. Propositions 6.2 and 6.6 show that the associated continuous extensions are uniquely determined by these restrictions. Moreover, Proposition 6.8 iden-tifies the tangent cone of the convex hull of an M-convex set with the cone generated by the root directions along which one can move one step within the set. This relates the geometry of the convex hull to the discrete structure of the M-convex set. 

• We establish the fundamental optimality and conjugacy properties of the resulting discrete functions. Local optimality guarantees global optimality for both L- and M-convex func-tions (Theorems 6.5 and 6.10). We also prove that integral L-convex functions and integral M-convex functions correspond one-to-one through the discrete Fenchel–Legendre conjugate 

(Theorem 6.11). This correspondence combines the uniqueness of the continuous exten-sions with the fact that continuous conjugacy exchanges the primal- and dual-integrality structures. 

• We present intrinsic combinatorial characterizations of the discrete convex notions intro-duced above. L-convex sets and functions are characterized by the discrete midpoint con-vexity with respect to the Euclidean Coxeter complex (Theorems 7.1 and 7.2), whereas M-convex sets and functions are characterized by the discrete tangent-cone property (The-orems 7.5 and 7.7). These characterizations are stated in discrete terms, except for the hole-freeness condition that appears in the characterizations of M-convexity. Finally, Sec-tion 8 identifies the resulting classes with, or precisely compares them to, several existing notions associated with the classical root systems. Some of the polyhedral notions introduced above recover existing Coxeter-theoretic objects. The L- and M-convex cones considered here coincide with Coxeter cones [64] and Coxeter root cones [2], respectively, while the M-convex polytopes coincide with generalized Coxeter permu-tahedra [2]. The contribution of the present paper is to introduce the discrete domains and notions of integrality needed to define L- and M-convex functions for classical root systems, and to organize these objects into a common framework for DCA. The assumption of classical type is needed only in a limited part of the theory. The type-dependent arguments are essentially confined to Propositions 5.7 and 6.8. In particular, most of the results on L-convexity, as well as the discrete conjugacy theorem, remain valid for arbitrary irreducible root systems, including the exceptional types; see Remark 5.1. 

Organization. The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Section 2 summarizes the pre-liminary concepts and notations concerning simplicial complexes, vector spaces, and continuous convex analysis. Section 3 introduces the definitions and basic properties of root systems, includ-ing their classification into classical types. Section 4 details the combinatorial structures arising from root systems, such as root lattices and spherical/Euclidean Coxeter complexes, along with their geometric realizations and Lov´ asz extensions. In Section 5, we formulate discrete con-vexity in Euclidean spaces by defining L- and M-convex cones, polyhedra, sublinear functions, 5and locally polyhedral convex functions. Building upon these geometric concepts, Section 6 formally defines L- and M-convex sets and functions over combinatorial objects and establishes the conjugacy between integral L- and M-convex functions. Section 7 provides combinatorial characterizations for these discrete convexities. These three sections constitute the main line of the paper. To make this line of argument easier to follow, we defer to Appendix A the proofs of statements that are not used in the arguments through Section 7, together with related discus-sions. In Section 8, we place our discrete convexity notions in relation to several existing classes, including the standard DCA, (valuated) ∆-matroids, bisubmodular functions, BS-convex sets and functions, UJ-convex functions, and Coxeter matroids/submodular functions. Finally, Sec-tion 9 concludes the paper with a summary of our results and a discussion of future research directions. 

# 2 Preliminaries 

Let Z and R denote the sets of integers and reals, respectively, and Z+ and R+ their nonnegative subsets. For a positive integer c, we define cZ := {cx | x ∈ Z} and Z/c := {x/c | x ∈ Z}.In particular, 2 Z is the set of even integers and Z/2 is the union of the sets of integers and of half-integers. Let 2 Z + 1 and Z + 1 /2 be the sets of odd integers and of half-integers, respectively. Let S be a set. The number of elements in S is denoted by # S, which is in Z+ ∪ { +∞} .For s ∈ S and a tuple x ∈ RS of reals indexed by S, we denote by x(s) its sth component. Similarly, for x ∈ Rn and i ∈ [n], we denote by x(i) its ith component. For x ∈ RS , its support 

supp x is the set of indices s with x(s)̸ = 0. For nonnegative integers m, n with m ≤ n, let [m, n ] := {m, m + 1 , . . . , n }. If m = 1 and n ≥ 1, then we abbreviate [ m, n ] as [ n]. 

2.1 Simplicial complex 

A partially ordered set (or poset ) is a pair (Σ , ⪯) of a set Σ and a binary relation ⪯ over Σ satisfying, for x, y, z ∈ Σ, that x ⪯ x (reflexivity), x ⪯ y and y ⪯ x imply x = y (antisymmetry), and x ⪯ y and y ⪯ z imply x ⪯ z (transitivity). By x ≺ y we mean x ⪯ y and x̸ = y. By just Σ we sometimes mean a poset if it is clear from the context. For a finite set S, the pair (2 S , ⊆)of the power set 2 S of S and the inclusion relation ⊆ forms a poset, which is called the Boolean lattice .A nonempty poset (Σ , ⪯) is called an (abstract) simplicial complex if all A, B ∈ Σ have their greatest lower bound, denoted by A ∧ B, and for all A ∈ Σ, there exists a unique nonnegative integer rA ∈ Z+ such that the subposet induced by Σ ⪯A := {B ∈ Σ | B ⪯ A} is isomorphic to the Boolean lattice (2 [rA], ⊆). A member A of Σ is called a simplex , and its rank is rA. In particular, a rank-one simplex is called a vertex . The rank of the lowest element of Σ, denoted by ⊥, is 0. Let (Σ , ⪯) be a simplicial complex. For A ∈ Σ, a simplex B with B ⪯ A is called a face 

of A. By a vertex of a simplex A we mean a rank-one face of A. Let V(Σ) denote the set of vertices of Σ, and V(A) the set of vertices of a simplex A; the number of the vertices of A is equal to the rank of A. Simplices A1, A 2, . . . , A k ∈ Σ are said to be joinable if there is a simplex 

A ∈ Σ such that all of A1, A 2, . . . , A k are faces of A, or equivalently, there is an upper bound of 

A1, A 2, . . . , A k in Σ. If A1, A 2, . . . , A k ∈ Σ are joinable, then their least upper bound exists; we refer to it as the join of A1, A 2, . . . , A k. Two simplices A, B are said to be disjoint if A ∧ B = ⊥.Two maximal simplices C, C ′ are said to be adjacent if they have a common corank-one face. A sequence ( C0, C 1, . . . , C ℓ) of maximal simplices is called a gallery if Ci−1 and Ci are adjacent for each i ∈ [ℓ]. If A ⪯ C0 and B ⪯ Cℓ for two simplices A, B ∈ Σ, then we refer to ( C0, C 1, . . . , C ℓ)as a gallery from A to B. The length of a gallery ( C0, C 1, . . . , C ℓ) is ℓ. A gallery from A to B

6is said to be minimal if its length is minimum among all galleries from A to B. The distance 

between A and B, denoted by d(A, B ), is defined by the length of a minimal gallery from A to 

B.A nonempty subset Σ ′ ⊆ Σ is called a subcomplex of Σ if A ∈ Σ′ and B ⪯ A imply B ∈ Σ′,i.e., Σ ′ is closed under ⪯. Note that Σ ′ is also a simplicial complex. For A ∈ Σ, we define the subcomplexes St Σ(A) and Lk Σ(A) of Σ by St Σ(A) := {B ∈ Σ | A and B are joinable },

Lk Σ(A) := {B ∈ Σ | A and B are disjoint and joinable }.

St Σ(A) is called the star complex and Lk Σ(A) the link complex . For a subset D ⊆ V (Σ), we denote by Σ[ D] the subcomplex of Σ induced by D, i.e., Σ[ D] := {A ∈ Σ | V (A) ⊆ D}.

Note that, if D = ∅, then Σ[ D] consists of the lowest element ⊥.A simplicial complex Σ is called a chamber complex if all maximal simplices have the same rank and any two maximal simplices are connected by a gallery. Let Σ be a chamber complex. Then, the distance d(A, B ) is finite for any two cells A, B ∈ Σ. A maximal simplex in Σ is called a chamber . The rank of Σ is the rank of a chamber. Suppose that the rank of Σ is n. Then, every chamber contains n vertices. Let I be a set with # I = n. A function τ : V(Σ) → I is called a type function on Σ if τ (C) coincides with I

for any chamber C ∈ Σ, where τ (A) := {τ (x) | x ∈ V (A)} for A ∈ Σ. In other words, a type function τ is a (proper) n-coloring of the 1-skeleton of Σ in the graphical sense. We refer to τ (A)as the type of a simplex A with respect to τ . We remark that # τ (A) coincides with the rank of 

A. A chamber complex Σ is said to be colorable if Σ admits a type function. For any simplex 

A of a colorable complex Σ with type function τ : V(Σ) → I, the map Σ ⪯A → 2τ (A), given by B 7 → τ (B) forms an isomorphism between the subposet (Σ ⪯A, ⪯) and the Boolean lattice (2 τ (A), ⊆). Let Σ be a colorable chamber complex of rank n and I a set of n elements, and choose an arbitrary chamber C ∈ Σ. Then, any bijection τC : V(C) → I can be uniquely extended to a type function τ : V(Σ) → I satisfying τ (x) = τC (x) for all x ∈ V (C). Indeed, since Σ is a colorable chamber complex, there is a type function τ : V(Σ) → I satisfying τ (x) = τC (x) for all 

x ∈ V (C). For any chamber D adjacent to C, we have |V (C) \ V (D)| = |V (D) \ V (C)| = 1; let 

xC and xD be the unique elements in V(C) \ V (D) and in V(D) \ V (C), respectively. Then, the type of xD must coincide with that of xC , i.e., τ (xD) = τ (xC ) = τC (xC ). By a similar argument with the fact that there is a gallery from C to any vertex x ∈ V (Σ), the type of every vertex x

is uniquely determined. 

2.2 Vector space 

In this paper, let V denote a finite-dimensional vector space over R with an inner product 

⟨· , ·⟩ : V × V → R. Although we may identify V with its dual vector space V ∗ via ⟨· , ·⟩ , we basically distinguish between V and V ∗. On the other hand, we always identify V with V ∗∗ in a canonical way. Both V and V ∗ are metric spaces induced from the norm ∥ · ∥ := ⟨· , ·⟩ .For a vector x ∈ V , we denote by x∗ the corresponding dual vector in V ∗ with respect to ⟨· , ·⟩ , that is, x∗(y) = ⟨x, y ⟩ for y ∈ V . Similarly, for a dual vector p ∈ V ∗, we denote by p∗ the corresponding primal vector in V ; p(y) = ⟨p∗, y ⟩ for y ∈ V . Let O( V ) and O( V ∗)denote the orthogonal groups over V and V ∗ with respect to ⟨· , ·⟩ , respectively. That is, we have 

⟨x, y ⟩ = ⟨wx, wy ⟩ for any x, y ∈ V and w ∈ O( V ). In this paper, we basically regard the inner 7product as the duality pairing ⟨· , ·⟩ : V ∗ × V → R over V ∗ and V given by ⟨p, x ⟩ := p(x), i.e., for 

p ∈ V ∗ and x ∈ V , we have p(x) = ⟨p∗, x ⟩ = ⟨p, x ⟩.By U ≤ V we mean that U is a vector subspace of V . For U ≤ V , the orthogonal space of 

U with respect to ⟨· , ·⟩ is denoted by U ⊥, that is, U ⊥ := {p ∈ V ∗ | ⟨ p, x ⟩ = 0 ( ∀x ∈ U )}. The equality ( U ⊥)⊥ = U holds. Let V /U denote the quotient space of V by U . Then, the duality pairing ⟨· , ·⟩ over V ∗ and V naturally induces that over U ⊥ and V /U , since ⟨p, x + U ⟩ := ⟨p, x ⟩

is well defined for p ∈ U ⊥ and x ∈ V , where x + U denotes the equivalence class containing x.Thus, ( V /U )∗ is isometric to U ⊥ for U ≤ V , and hence we identify ( V /U )∗ with U ⊥. Similarly, the duality pairing over V / (W ⊥) and W is naturally induced by ⟨· , ·⟩ as ⟨p, x + W ⊥⟩ := ⟨p, x ⟩ for 

p ∈ W and x ∈ V ; we also identify V / (W ⊥) with W ∗ for W ≤ V ∗.For a subset X ⊆ V , we denote by span X the linear hull of X in V , and by aff X the affine hull of X in V . For any affine subspace A of V , there exists a unique linear subspace A0

that is obtained by a parallel transformation of A, i.e., A = A0 + x for x ∈ A. For notational simplicity, let V / aff X denote V / (aff X)0, where (aff X)0 is the unique linear subspace of V that is parallel to aff X. For a subset X, Y ⊆ V , we denote by X + Y and X − Y the Minkowski sum and difference of X and Y , respectively. That is, X + Y := {x + y | x ∈ X, y ∈ Y } and 

X − Y := {x − y | x ∈ X, y ∈ Y }. In particular, if X = {x} (resp. Y = {y}), then we use x + Y

(resp. X − y) instead of {x} + Y (resp. X − { y}). Let cl X and relint X denote the closure and the relative interior of X, respectively. For x, y ∈ V , let [ x, y ] := {λx + (1 − λ)y | λ ∈ [0 , 1] } and (x, y ) := {λx + (1 − λ)y | λ ∈ (0 , 1) } denote the closed and open line segments connecting x and 

y, respectively. In the case of V = Rn, we assume that the inner product ⟨· , ·⟩ is given by ⟨x, y ⟩ = Pni=1 x(i)y(i)for x, y ∈ V , and identify its dual ( Rn)∗ with Rn. Let 1n denote the all-one vector in Rn. The 

ith unit vector in Rn is denoted by ei.

2.3 Basics of convex analysis 

Let f : V → R ∪ {±∞} be a function. The sets dom f := {x ∈ V | − ∞ < f (x) < +∞} ,

epi f := {(x, y ) ∈ V × R | f (x) ≤ y}

are called the effective domain and the epigraph of f , respectively. If epi f is a closed set in 

V × R, then f is said to be closed . A set C ⊆ V is called a cone if μx ∈ C holds for x ∈ C and 

μ > 0. A function f is said to be positively homogeneous if f (μx ) = μf (x) holds for any x ∈ V

and μ > 0, or equivalently, its epigraph epi f forms a cone. For a function g : V → R ∪ {±∞} , we say that f majorizes g, or g minorizes f if g ≤ f , i.e., g(x) ≤ f (x) for all x ∈ V . If f majorizes some affine function, then f cannot take −∞ , namely, f is a function from V to R ∪ { +∞} .A set C ⊆ V is said to be convex if x, y ∈ C implies λx + (1 − λ)y ∈ C for all λ ∈ (0 , 1). A function f : V → R ∪ {±∞} is said to be convex if λf (x) + (1 − λ)f (y) ≥ f (λx + (1 − λ)y) holds for any distinct x, y ∈ V and λ ∈ (0 , 1). It is well known that f is a convex function if and only if its epigraph epi f is a convex set. A convex function f is said to be proper if f never takes 

−∞ and dom f is nonempty. A convex and positively homogeneous function is referred to as a 

sublinear function , which is characterized as a function whose epigraph is a convex cone. Let C ⊆ V be a convex set. A convex subset F ⊆ C is called a face (see e.g., [60, Section 18]) of C if x, y ∈ C and λx + (1 − λ)y ∈ F for some λ ∈ (0 , 1) imply x, y ∈ F , and called an exposed face if there is p ∈ V ∗ such that F = argmin x∈C ⟨p, x ⟩. Any exposed face is a face, but the converse does not necessarily hold. A face F of C is said to be proper if F̸ = ∅ and F̸ = C. The face relation is transitive; for a face F of C, a subset F ′ ⊆ F is a face of F if and only if F ′ is a face of C. The family consisting of the relative interiors of all faces of C forms a partition of 8C [60, Theorem 18.2]. That is, for any point x in a convex set C, there exists a unique face F

of C such that x ∈ relint F .For a closed convex set C ⊆ V and a point x ∈ C, the tangent cone Tx(C) of C at x is defined by T x(C) := cl(cone {y − x | y ∈ C}). We can easily derive the following by fundamental arguments in convex analysis. 

Lemma 2.1. For a nonempty closed convex set C ⊆ V , we have C = T 

> x∈C

(x + T x(C)) .Proof. The inclusion C ⊆ T 

> x∈C

(x + T x(C)) is immediate. Conversely, let y /∈ C. By the separation theorem (see e.g., [34, Corollary A.4.2.4]), there are ˆ x ∈ C and p ∈ V ∗ such that 

⟨p, y ⟩ < ⟨p, ˆx⟩ ≤ ⟨ p, x ⟩ for all x ∈ C. Hence, ⟨p, d ⟩ ≥ 0 for every d ∈ Tˆx(C), and therefore 

y / ∈ ˆx + T ˆx(C). 

2.3.1 Convex and conical hull 

For a set S, let Λ S and MS denote the sets of all convex combination coefficients ( λ(s)) s∈S over 

S and of all conical combination coefficients ( μ(s)) s∈S over S, respectively. That is, ΛS := 

(

For a subset X ⊆ V , we denote by conv X and cone X the convex hull and conical hull of X,respectively: conv X := 

( X

Note that conv ∅ = ∅ and cone ∅ = {0}. The closed convex hull of X, denoted by conv X, is the closure of conv X. The (closed) convex hull of X can be characterized as the minimum (closed) convex set that contains X.For a function f : V → R ∪ { +∞} , its convex hull conv f and closed convex hull conv f are defined by (conv f )( x) := inf 

X

If f majorizes some affine function, then the (closed) convex hull of f can be characterized as the pointwise maximum (closed) convex function that minorizes f ; particularly, conv f ≤ conv f ≤ f holds. The following lemma shows the relationship between the effective domains of a function and its (closed) convex hulls, in which the identity conv(dom f ) = dom(conv f ) immediately follows from the definition of the convex hull. 

Lemma 2.2 ([60, Corollary 7.4.1]) . Let f : V → R ∪ { +∞} be a function minorized by some affine function. Then, we have conv(dom f ) = dom(conv f ) ⊆ dom(conv f ) ⊆ conv(dom f ).

The conical hull of a function f : V → R ∪ { +∞} , denoted by cone f , is defined by (cone f )( x) := inf

If f majorizes some linear function, then the conical hull of f can be characterized as the pointwise maximum proper sublinear function that minorizes f .If f does not majorize any linear function, then (cone f )( x) = −∞ for all x ∈ cone(dom f ). 

2.3.2 Fenchel–Legendre conjugate 

For a function f : V → R ∪ { +∞} that majorizes some affine function, its Fenchel–Legendre conjugate f ∗, which is a function on V ∗, is defined by 

f ∗(p) := sup {⟨ p, x ⟩ − f (x) | x ∈ V } (p ∈ V ∗).

The Fenchel–Legendre conjugate provides the one-to-one correspondence between the proper closed convex functions on V and those on V ∗, which plays a central role in convex analysis. 

Lemma 2.3 (see e.g., [34, Theorems E.1.1.2 and E.1.3.5]) . Let f : V → R ∪ { +∞} be a proper function that majorizes some affine function. 

(1) f ∗ is a closed convex function on V ∗.

(2) conv f = f ∗∗ . In particular, if f is a closed convex function, then f = f ∗∗ .

As a special case of the above, we obtain the following one-to-one correspondence between the nonempty closed convex sets and the proper closed sublinear functions. For a set S ⊆ V , its 

support function σ[S] is the function on V ∗ defined by 

σ[S]( p) := sup {⟨ p, x ⟩ | x ∈ S} (p ∈ V ∗),

and for a function σ on V , we define the subset S(σ) of V ∗ by 

S(σ) := {p ∈ V ∗ | ⟨ p, x ⟩ ≤ σ(x) ( ∀x ∈ V )}.

Lemma 2.4 ([34, Theorem C.2.2.2]) . For a nonempty closed convex set S and a proper closed sublinear function σ, we have S = S(σ[S]) and σ = σ[S(σ)] . In addition, for any set S, its support function σ[S] is a closed sublinear function and conv S = S(σ[S]) , i.e., 

conv S = {p ∈ V ∗ | ⟨ p, x ⟩ ≤ σ[S]( x) ( ∀x ∈ V )}.

2.3.3 Directional derivative and subdifferential 

Let f be a function on V and x ∈ dom f . The directional derivative of f at x with respect to 

d ∈ V , denoted by f ′(x; d), is the limit 

f ′(x; d) := lim 

> t↓0

f (x + td ) − f (x)

t

if it exists (it can take + ∞ or −∞ ). If f ′(x; d) exists for all d ∈ V , then we refer to the function 

f ′(x; ·) : V → R ∪ { +∞, −∞} as the directional derivative of f at x. The subdifferential of f at 

x, denoted by ∂f (x), is the subset of V ∗ defined by 

∂f (x) := {p ∈ V ∗ | f (y) ≥ f (x) + ⟨p, y − x⟩ (∀y ∈ V )}

= {p ∈ V ∗ | x ∈ argmin( f − p)}.

A point p ∈ ∂f (x) is called a subgradient of f at x. If f : V → R ∪ { +∞} is a proper convex function, then, for each x ∈ dom f , the directional derivative f ′(x; ·) (exists and) forms a sublinear function. Specifically, the following relations hold [60, Theorem 23.2]: conv ( f ′(x; ·)) = σ[∂f (x)] , (2.4) 

∂f (x) = S(f ′(x; ·)) . (2.5) 10 2.3.4 The minimizer sets with perturbations 

For a function f : V → R ∪ { +∞} and x ∈ dom f , we define Π( f ) := {argmin( f − p) | p ∈ V ∗, argmin( f − p)̸ = ∅} ,

Π( f ; x) := {S ∈ Π( f ) | x ∈ S}.

We note that, if f is a convex function, then any member of Π( f ) can be viewed as the projection of an exposed face of epi f ⊆ V × R to V . If f is a closed convex function, then every member of Π( f ) is a closed convex set. We also define the supports of Π( f ) and Π( f ; x) by 

|Π( f )| := [

respectively. We here summarize easy observations on Π( f ) of a convex function f .

Lemma 2.5. Let f : V → R ∪ { +∞} be a proper convex function. 

(1) relint(dom f ) ⊆ | Π( f )| ⊆ dom f . In particular, if |Π( f )| is closed, then |Π( f )| = dom f .

(2) For S, T ∈ Π( f ), we have that S is a face of T if and only if (relint S) ∩ T̸ = ∅.

(3) If there is a subset Π′ ⊆ Π( f ) such that dom f = S 

> S∈Π′

S, then |Π( f )| = dom f and any member of Π( f ) is a face of some member of Π′.

(4) Let x ∈ dom f and C ∈ Π( f ) such that x ∈ relint C. For any y ∈ C, we have S(f ′(x; ·)) ⊆

S(f ′(y; ·)) .

(5) If f is a proper closed convex function, then argmin( f ∗ − x) = S(f ′(x; ·)) for x ∈ dom f

and argmin( f − p) = S(( f ∗)′(p; ·)) for p ∈ dom f ∗. In particular, we have

Proof. (1). The inclusion |Π( f )| ⊆ dom f is immediate. By [60, Theorem 23.4], every point in relint(dom f ) admits a subgradient, and hence belongs to some member of Π( f ). (2) follows from [60, Theorem 18.1 and Corollary 18.1.2], applied to the exposed faces of epi f

corresponding to the members of Π( f ). (3) follows from the assertions (1) and (2). (4). If p ∈ ∂f (x), i.e., x ∈ argmin( f − p) ∈ Π( f ), then C is a face of argmin( f − p) by the assertion (2). Thus, every y ∈ C belongs to argmin( f − p), i.e., p ∈ ∂f (y). Therefore, we obtain 

∂f (x) ⊆ ∂f (y), which is equivalent to S(f ′(x; ·)) ⊆ S(f ′(y; ·)) by (2.5). (5) follows from [60, Theorem 23.5 and Corollary 23.5.1] with the equation (2.5). 

2.3.5 Polyhedral structures 

A subset P ⊆ V is called a polyhedron (see e.g., [60, Theorem 19.1]) if it is representable as the intersection of finitely many closed half-spaces of V , i.e., 

P = S(θ) = {x ∈ V | ⟨ p, x ⟩ ≤ θ(p) ( p ∈ dom θ)} (2.6) for some θ : V ∗ → R ∪ { +∞} such that dom θ is a finite set, or equivalently, it is representable as the Minkowski sum of the convex hull of finitely many points in V and the conical hull of finitely many vectors in V , i.e., 

P = conv X + cone Y (2.7) 11 for some finite sets X, Y ⊆ V ; the second summand cone Y in (2.7) is uniquely determined by 

P and is called the recession cone (or characteristic cone ) of P . We refer to the former repre-sentation of a polyhedron P as an outer-representation and the latter an inner-representation .In the inner-representation (2.7), P̸ = ∅ if and only if X̸ = ∅. A bounded polyhedron is called a polytope . A polyhedral cone is a cone that is also a polyhedron; an inner-representation of a polyhedral cone is of the form cone Y for some finite set Y ⊆ V , and an outer-representation is of the form {x ∈ V | ⟨ p, x ⟩ ≤ 0 ( ∀p ∈ F )} for some finite set F ⊆ V ∗.For a cone K ⊆ V , its polar cone K∗ ⊆ V ∗ is 

K∗ := {p ∈ V ∗ | ⟨ p, x ⟩ ≤ 0 ( ∀x ∈ K)},

which forms a closed convex cone. If K is a closed convex cone then K = K∗∗ , and if K is a polyhedral cone then so is K∗. From an outer-representation (resp. an inner-representation) of a polyhedral cone, we can easily construct an inner-representation (resp. an outer-representation) of its polar cone as follows. 

Lemma 2.6 (Farkas lemma; see e.g., [34, Examples A.3.2.2] and [60, Corollary 22.3.1]) . If K is a polyhedral cone of the form {x ∈ V | ⟨ p, x ⟩ ≤ 0 ( ∀p ∈ Q)} for some finite set Q ⊆ V ∗, then its polar cone K∗ is representable as K∗ = cone Q.

Let P be a polyhedron. For x ∈ P , its tangent cone T x(P ) of P at x forms a polyhedral cone representable as T x(P ) = cone {d ∈ V | ∃ ε > 0. x + εd ∈ P }. For x ∈ P , let Px denote the unique face of P such that x ∈ relint( Px). The following is a fundamental fact on the tangent cone of a polyhedron. 

Lemma 2.7 (see e.g., [4, Proposition 3.5.2]) . For a polyhedron P and x, y ∈ P with Px = Py,we have Tx(P ) = T y(P ).

Since the number of faces of P is finite (see e.g., [61, Section 8.3]), so is the set {Tx(P ) | x ∈ P }

by Lemma 2.7, which consists of polyhedral cones. We can see that this property ensures a closed convex set to be a polyhedron. 

Lemma 2.8. For a closed convex set C, it is a polyhedron if and only if {Tx(C) | x ∈ C} is a finite set consisting of polyhedral cones. Proof. We only show the if part; we have already seen the only-if part above. For x ∈ C, since Tx(C) is a polyhedral cone, there is a finite set Sx ⊆ V ∗ such that T x(C) = {y ∈ V | ⟨ p, y ⟩ ≤ 

0 ( ∀p ∈ Sx)}; we may assume that Sx = Sy if T x(C) = T y(C). Then, since {Tx(C) | x ∈ C}

is finite, so is the set S := S 

> x∈C

Sx. For each p ∈ S and x ∈ C, let rp(x) := ⟨p, x ⟩ if p ∈ Sx,and rp(x) := + ∞ if p / ∈ Sx. Then, x + T x(C) = {y ∈ V | ⟨ p, y ⟩ ≤ rp(x) ( ∀p ∈ S)} holds. Since 

C = T 

> x∈C

(x + T x(C)) (Lemma 2.1), we obtain C = {y ∈ V | ⟨ p, y ⟩ ≤ inf x∈C rp(x) ( ∀p ∈ S)}.The finiteness of S implies that C is a polyhedron. A function is said to be polyhedral convex if its epigraph forms a polyhedron. In particular, a polyhedral sublinear function is a function whose epigraph forms a polyhedral cone. We note that the effective domain of a polyhedral convex function (resp. polyhedral sublinear function) forms a polyhedron (resp. a polyhedral cone). A proper polyhedral sublinear function σ admits two kinds of representations similar to a polyhedron (see (2.6) and (2.7)); σ is always representable as 

σ(p) = (cone θ)( p) = inf 

X

σ(p) = 

(

max {⟨ p, x ⟩ | x ∈ X} if p ∈ (cone Y )∗ = {q ∈ V ∗ | ⟨ q, y ⟩ ≤ 0 ( ∀y ∈ Y )},

+∞ otherwise (2.9) for some finite sets X, Y ⊆ V . The former and latter representations of σ are referred to as an 

inner- and outer-representation , respectively. The specialization of the one-to-one correspondence between the nonempty closed convex sets and the proper closed sublinear functions (Lemma 2.4) to the polyhedral case provides the one-to-one correspondence between the nonempty polyhedra and the proper polyhedral sublinear functions [60, Theorem 19.2 and Corollary 19.2.1]. More specifically, from an outer-representation (resp. an inner-representation) of a polyhedron, we can easily construct an inner-representation (resp. an outer-representation) of its support function, and vice versa, as follows. 

Lemma 2.9. Let P be a nonempty polyhedron of the form (2.6) (resp. (2.7) ) and σ a proper polyhedral sublinear function of the form (2.8) (resp. (2.9) ). Then, we have P = S(σ) and 

σ = σ[P ]. In addition, for p ∈ dom σ, the set {x ∈ P | ⟨ p, x ⟩ = σ(p)} is a nonempty face of P .

A family Π of polyhedra is called a polyhedral complex if it satisfies that (i) if P ∈ Π and P ′ is a face of P then P ′ ∈ Π; and (ii) the nonempty intersection P ∩ Q of two members P, Q ∈ Π is a face of P and Q.A polyhedral complex consisting of polyhedral cones is particularly called a polyhedral fan . The 

support of a polyhedral complex Π, denoted by |Π|, is defined as S  

> P∈Π

P . For example, for a polyhedral convex function f and a polyhedral sublinear function σ, the families Π( f ) and Π( σ) form a polyhedral complex whose support is dom f and a polyhedral fan whose support is dom σ, respectively (see e.g., [68, Chapter 5] and [29]). We can easily obtain the following from Lemma 2.9, which is a special case of Lemma 2.5 (5). 

Lemma 2.10. Let P ⊆ V be a nonempty polyhedron and σ : V ∗ → R ∪ { +∞} a proper poly-hedral sublinear function such that P = S(σ), namely, σ = σ[P ]. Then, we have Π( σ) = 

{(T x(P )) ∗ | x ∈ P } and Tx(P ) = (argmin( σ − x)) ∗ for x ∈ P .

A convex set Q ⊆ V is said to be locally polyhedral if, for any polytope P , the intersection 

Q∩P forms a polytope. Similarly, a function is said to be locally polyhedral convex if its epigraph forms a locally polyhedral set, or equivalently, if for any polytope P , the restriction of f to P is a polyhedral convex function. We can easily see that locally polyhedral convex sets and functions are closed. The definition of locally polyhedral convex sets (resp. functions) states that a locally poly-hedral convex set (resp. function) behaves locally like a polyhedron (resp. polyhedral convex function). Hence, many local properties of polyhedral structures can be generalized to the case of locally polyhedral structures. We summarize basic (local) properties of a locally polyhedral convex function as follows. 

Lemma 2.11. Let f : V → R ∪ { +∞} be a locally polyhedral convex function. 

(1) dom f = |Π( f )|.

(2) f ′(x; ·) is a proper polyhedral sublinear function and ∂f (x) is a nonempty polyhedron for 

x ∈ dom f . In addition, Π( f ′(x; ·)) = {Tx(Q) | Q ∈ Π( f ; x)} and f ′(x; d) = f (x + d) − f (x)

for x ∈ dom f and d ∈ | Π( f ; x)| − x.

13 (3) If every member of Π( f ) is a polyhedron, then Π( f ) forms a polyhedral complex. Proof. (1) and (2). Fix any x ∈ dom f and polytope P ⊆ V with x ∈ int P . Then, the restriction f |P of f to P is a polyhedral convex function such that f ′(x; ·) = ( f |P )′(x; ·) (and hence 

∂f (x) = ∂f |P (x)). It follows from [60, Theorem 23.10] that ( f |P )′(x; ·) is a proper polyhedral convex function and ∂f |P (x) is a nonempty polyhedron. Hence so are f ′(x; ·) and ∂f (x), which implies the assertion (1) and the former statement of the assertion (2). The latter statement follows from the linearity of f and f ′(x; ·) on Q ∈ Π( f ; x). (3). For any convex function f , the family Π( f ) generally satisfies the condition (ii) in the definition of a polyhedral complex. Indeed, for any Q = argmin( f − p), Q ′ = argmin( f − p′) ∈

Π( f ) with Q∩Q′̸ = ∅, we have Q∩Q′ = argmin( f −(p+p′)/2), which implies that Q∩Q′ ∈ Π( f ). Hence, it suffices to see that any nonempty face F of Q ∈ Π( f ) belongs to Π( f ). Take any Q ∈ Π( f ) and its nonempty face F . Let P be a full-dimensional polytope in V such that P ∩ relint F̸ = ∅. Since f is a locally polyhedral convex function, the restriction f |P of f to 

P is a polyhedral convex function, and hence, Π( f |P ) is a polyhedral complex containing P ∩ Q.Since F is a face of Q and P ∩ relint F̸ = ∅, P ∩ F is a nonempty face of P ∩ Q, which implies that 

P ∩ F ∈ Π( f |P ). Thus, there is p ∈ V ∗ such that P ∩ F = argmin( f |P − p). For such p, we have 

P ∩ F ⊆ argmin( f − p). Hence, argmin( f − p) ∩ relint F̸ = ∅ holds, which implies that F is a face of argmin( f − p). Since dim( P ∩ F ) = dim F = dim(argmin( f − p)) by the full-dimensionality of P , we obtain F = argmin( f − p), that is, F ∈ Π( f ). This completes the proof. 

2.3.6 Integrality of polyhedra 

A subset L ⊆ V ∗ is called a lattice if there is a basis {p1, p 2, . . . , p n} of V ∗ such that L is representable as 

L =

( nX

> i=1

cipi c1, c 2, . . . , c n ∈ Z

)

. (2.10) We particularly say that the lattice of the form (2.10) is generated from {p1, p 2, . . . , p n} and that 

{p1, p 2, . . . , p n} is an L-basis (or just a basis ). A polyhedron P ⊆ V ∗ is said to be L-integral if P = conv( P ∩ L ) holds. There are several characterizations of the ( Zn-)integrality of polyhedra, which are well known in the field of combi-natorial optimization (see e.g., [61, Section 16.3]). By adapting the proof there, we immediately obtain the following. 

Lemma 2.12 (see e.g., [61, Section 16.3]) . Let L be a lattice in V ∗ and P ⊆ V ∗ a polyhedron. Then, P is L-integral if and only if P = conv X + cone Y for some finite sets X, Y ⊆ L . In addition, if P is L-integral, then so is every nonempty face of P .

# 3 Root system 

In this section, we review the fundamental concepts and basic properties of root systems. Sec-tion 3.1 provides the formal definition of a root system and introduces the notion of (extended) simple systems . Section 3.2 introduces Weyl groups and (extended) Dynkin diagrams , which capture the geometric and algebraic symmetries of root systems. Finally, Section 3.3 reviews the classification of irreducible root systems, with a particular focus on the classical types A, B, C, and D. 14 3.1 Definitions 

For a nonzero dual vector α ∈ V ∗ \ { 0}, let α∨ be a vector in V defined by 

α∨ := 2

⟨α, α ∗⟩ α∗; (3.1) we can easily see that ⟨α, α ∨⟩ = 2. We similarly define x∨ ∈ V ∗ for a nonzero vector x ∈ V \ { 0}.Then, we have ( α∨)∨ = α. The reflection sα with respect to α is a linear map in V defined by 

sα(x) := x − ⟨ α, x ⟩α∨ (x ∈ V ).

That is, sα is the reflection with respect to the hyperplane in V orthogonal to α. By abuse of notation, we assume that sα also represents the reflection in V ∗ with respect to α∨:

sα(p) := p − ⟨ p, α ∨⟩α (p ∈ V ∗). (3.2) A nonempty finite set Φ ⊆ V ∗ \ { 0} of nonzero dual vectors is called a root system [27, Definition 8.1] if it satisfies the following conditions (R1)–(R3): (R1) sα(β) ∈ Φ for any α, β ∈ Φ; (R2) {λα | λ ∈ R} ∩ Φ = {α, −α} for any α ∈ Φ; (R3) ⟨α, β ∨⟩ ∈ Z for any α, β ∈ Φ. A member α of Φ is called a root and α∨ a coroot . Let Φ ∨ := {α∨ | α ∈ Φ} ⊆ V . Then, Φ ∨ also satisfies (R1)–(R3), and is called the coroot system . A root system Φ is said to be essential if span Φ = V ∗.It is known [27, Proposition 8.6] that for any two distinct roots α, β ∈ Φ with ∥α∥ ≥ ∥ β∥ and 

α̸ = −β, one of the following (T0)–(T3) holds: (T0) The angle between α and β is π/ 2, or equivalently, ⟨α, β ∨⟩ = ⟨β, α ∨⟩ = 0; (T1) ∥α∥ = ∥β∥ and the angle between α and β is π/ 3 or 2 π/ 3, or equivalently, ⟨α, β ∨⟩ =

⟨β, α ∨⟩ = 1 or ⟨α, β ∨⟩ = ⟨β, α ∨⟩ = −1; (T2) ∥α∥ = √2∥β∥ and the angle between α and β is π/ 4 or 3 π/ 4, or equivalently, ⟨α, β ∨⟩ = 2 and ⟨β, α ∨⟩ = 1, or ⟨α, β ∨⟩ = −2 and ⟨β, α ∨⟩ = −1; (T3) ∥α∥ = √3∥β∥ and the angle between α and β is π/ 6 or 5 π/ 6, or equivalently, ⟨α, β ∨⟩ = 3 and ⟨β, α ∨⟩ = 1, or ⟨α, β ∨⟩ = −3 and ⟨β, α ∨⟩ = −1. If a root system Φ admits a nontrivial partition {Φ1, Φ2, . . . , Φk} such that, for any α ∈ Φi

and β ∈ Φj with distinct i, j , we have ⟨α, β ∨⟩ = 0, i.e., α and β satisfy (T0), then each of Φ1, Φ2, . . . , Φk is also a root system; in this case, Φ is called the direct sum of Φ 1, Φ2, . . . , Φk and is denoted by Φ 1 ⊕ Φ2 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Φk. A root system Φ is said to be reducible if Φ is the direct sum of at least two root systems, and irreducible if it is not reducible. If Φ = Φ 1 ⊕ Φ2 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Φk

for irreducible root systems Φ 1, Φ2, . . . , Φk, then each of Φ 1, Φ2, . . . , Φk is called an irreducible component of Φ. The set of irreducible components of Φ is uniquely determined. Two root systems Φ 1 and Φ 2 are said to be isomorphic if there exists an invertible linear map φ : V ∗ → V ∗

such that φ induces a bijection between Φ 1 and Φ 2 and for all α ∈ Φ1 and p ∈ V ∗, we have 

φ(sα(p)) = sφ(α)(φ(p)), i.e., the following diagram 

V ∗ V ∗

V ∗ V ∗ 

> sα
> φφsφ(α)

15 commutes. We write Φ 1 ≲ Φ2 if for each α ∈ Φ1, there exist μ ∈ R+ and α′ ∈ Φ2 such that 

α′ = μα . If Φ 1 ≲ Φ2 and Φ 1 ≳ Φ2, then we say that Φ 1 and Φ 2 are equivalent , and denoted by Φ1 ∼ Φ2.Let Φ be a root system. A subset ∆ ⊆ Φ is a simple system if ∆ is a basis of span Φ as a vector space and Φ = (Φ ∩ cone ∆) ∪ (Φ ∩ cone ( −∆)). We can easily see that, if Φ = Φ 1 ⊕ Φ2 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Φk

and ∆ i is a simple system of Φ i for each i, then ∆ = ∆ 1 ∪ ∆2 ∪ · · · ∪ ∆k is a simple system of Φ. Suppose that n := dim(span Φ) and that ∆ = {α1, α 2, . . . , α n} ⊆ Φ is a simple system. By the first condition in the definition of a root system, every vector p ∈ span Φ is uniquely representable as the linear combination of α1, α 2, . . . , α n; p = Pni=1 piαi for some unique p1, p 2, . . . , p n ∈ R.We introduce a partial order ≤∆ on V ∗ as: For p = Pni=1 piαi, q = Pni=1 qiαi ∈ V ∗, p ≤∆ q if and only if pi ≤ qi for all i ∈ [n]. The second condition in the definition of a simple system says that every root α ∈ Φ is either α > ∆ 0 or α < ∆ 0. The former root is said to be positive and the latter negative . An intriguing fact is that every root α ∈ Φ is representable as the linear combination of the simple roots with integral coefficients: 

Lemma 3.1 ([38, Chapter 2.9]) . Let Φ be a root system and {α1, α 2, . . . , α n} a simple system of Φ. For every root α ∈ Φ, there (uniquely) exist integers c1, c 2, . . . , c n ∈ Z such that α =Pni=1 ciαi.

Suppose that a root system Φ is irreducible. Then, for each simple system ∆ = {α1, α 2, . . . , α n},there exists a unique maximal element ˜ α ∈ Φ with respect to ≤∆, which is called the highest root with respect to ∆. The set ˜∆ := {α0, α 1, . . . , α n} that consists of the roots αi in a simple system ∆ = {α1, . . . , α n} and the negative α0 := −˜α of the highest root ˜ α with respect to ∆ is called an extended simple system . In the following, when we say that ˜∆ = {α0, α 1, . . . , α n} is an extended simple system, we assume that ∆ = {α1, . . . , α n} is a simple system of an irreducible root system and α0 is the negative of the highest root with respect to ∆. 

3.2 Weyl groups and (extended) Dynkin diagrams 

Let Φ be a root system. The group WΦ generated by the reflections sα with respect to the roots 

α ∈ Φ, i.e., 

WΦ := ⟨sα | α ∈ Φ⟩ (3.3) is called the Weyl group with respect to Φ. The Weyl group is viewed as the subgroup of O( V ) or O( V ∗) that depends on the context, i.e., this group naturally acts on V or V ∗ as WΦ ∋ w : v 7 →

wv . We can easily see that, if Φ = Φ 1 ⊕ Φ2 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Φk then WΦ = WΦ1 ⊕ WΦ2 ⊕ · · · ⊕ WΦk , and that if Φ 1 and Φ 2 are equivalent then WΦ1 = WΦ2 . The following lemma states that the Weyl group is generated by the reflections with respect to a simple system: 

Lemma 3.2 ([1, Theorem 1.69 (1)] and [27, Proposition 8.24]) . For an arbitrary simple system 

∆ of a root system Φ, we have WΦ = ⟨sα | α ∈ ∆⟩.

For S ⊆ V ∗ (or S ⊆ V ) and w ∈ WΦ, we denote {ws | s ∈ S} by wS . The following is an intriguing fact on the Weyl group. 

Lemma 3.3 ([1, Theorem 1.69 (2)]) . The Weyl group WΦ acts simply transitively on the set of simple systems, i.e., for any w ∈ WΦ and simple system ∆, the set w∆ is also a simple system, and for any two simple systems ∆, ∆′ of Φ, there exists a unique w ∈ WΦ such that ∆′ = w∆.The same holds for the extended simple systems. 

Thus, we obtain the one-to-one correspondence between the set of simple systems and the Weyl group WΦ, once we fix a simple system that corresponds to the identity element of WΦ.16 Let ∆ = {α1, α 2, . . . , α n} be a simple system of Φ. The Dynkin diagram D(Φ) of Φ is a mixed graph (a graph that may have edges and arcs) such that its vertex set is {v1, v 2, . . . , v n}

and for each pair of distinct vertices vi, v j ,(D0) there are no edges and arcs if αi and αj satisfy (T0), i.e., ⟨αi, α ∨

> j

⟩ = ⟨αj , α ∨ 

> i

⟩ = −1; (D2) there are two parallel arcs of the form ( vi, v j ) if αi and αj satisfy (T2), i.e., ⟨αi, α ∨

The extended Dynkin diagram ˜D(Φ) of an irreducible root system Φ is defined from an extended simple system ˜∆ = {α0, α 1, . . . , α n} of Φ similarly as the Dynkin diagram from a simple system ∆ with the following additional rule: (D ∞) There is an edge {vi, v j } with label ∞ if αi = −αj .The vertex set of ˜D(Φ) is {v0, v 1, . . . , v n} in which vi corresponds to αi and the edge set is similarly defined as that of the Dynkin diagram. For notational simplicity, we also denote by 

D(Φ) (resp. ˜D(Φ)) the vertex set of the Dynkin diagram D(Φ) (resp. the extended Dynkin diagram D(Φ)). The graph structures of D(Φ) and ˜D(Φ) are independent of the choice of a simple system by Lemma 3.3. In addition, they characterize the structure of a root system Φ. 

Lemma 3.4 ([27, Proposition 8.32]) . (1) Root systems Φ1 and Φ2 are isomorphic if and only if D(Φ 1) and D(Φ 2) are isomorphic in the graphical sense. 

(2) A root system is irreducible if and only if its Dynkin diagram is connected. 

Next, we introduce the type τ∆(α) of a root α in a simple system ∆ as follows. Suppose that D(Φ), whose vertex set is {v1, v 2, . . . , v n}, is constructed from a simple system ∆ =

{α1, α 2, . . . , α n} as in the definition of the Dynkin diagram. By Lemma 3.3, for any simple system ∆ ′, there exists a unique w ∈ WΦ such that ∆ ′ = w∆ = {wα 1, wα 2, . . . , wα n}. Hence, we can uniquely correspond wα i ∈ ∆′ to αi ∈ ∆, and hence to the vertex vi ∈ D(Φ). Then, we define τ∆′ (wα i) := vi, which is referred to as the type of wα i with respect to ∆′. Also, in the case of the extended Dynkin diagram ˜D(Φ), we can provide the one-to-one correspondence between wα 0, wα 1, wα 2, . . . , wα n ∈ w ˜∆ and v0, v 1, v 2, . . . , v n ∈ ˜D(Φ) in a similar manner; we define τw ˜∆(wα i) := vi, which is referred to as the type of wα i with respect to w ˜∆. For a simple system ∆ and vertex subset J ⊆ D(Φ), let ∆|J := {α ∈ ∆ | τ∆(α) ∈ J},

For a vertex vi of D(Φ) with τ∆(αi) = vi, we refer to the coefficient ai as the mark of vi.This is well defined, since for any simple system ∆ ′ of Φ, the coefficient of α′ ∈ ∆′ in the highest root with respect to ∆ ′ coincides with that of α ∈ ∆ if τ∆′ (α′) = τ∆(α). Similarly, we define the 

marks of vertices v1, v 2, . . . , v n of ˜D(Φ) as a1, a 2, . . . , a n, respectively. In addition, we define the 

mark a0 of v0 ∈ ˜D(Φ) \ D(Φ) (that corresponds to the negative of the highest root) as 1. Then, the equality Pni=0 aiαi = 0 holds. If Φ = Φ 1 ⊕ Φ2 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Φk is reducible, where Φ 1, Φ2, . . . , Φk

are irreducible components of Φ, then D(Φ) is the disjoint union of D(Φ 1), D (Φ 2), . . . , D (Φ k)(see Lemma 3.4 (2)); we define the mark of v ∈ D(Φ i) ⊆ D(Φ) as the mark of v with respect to 

D(Φ i). 

3.3 Classification of irreducible root systems 

It is known (see e.g., [38, Chapters 2 and 4]) that irreducible root systems have been completely classified as four infinite families A n, B n, C n (n ≥ 1), and D n (n ≥ 3) of root systems, called the 

classical root systems , and five exceptional ones E 6, E 7, E 8, F 4, and G 2, called the exceptional root systems . Here, the subscript of each of the letters A, B, C, D, E, F, and G describes the dimension of span Φ, or equivalently, the size of a simple system. Table 1 provides the (extended) Dynkin diagrams of the classical root systems of types A n,Bn, C n, and D n. The circles represent the vertices ( v0, )v1, . . . , v n of the (extended) Dynkin diagram, and a positive integer written inside a vertex represents its mark. In particular, in the extended Dynkin diagrams, the vertex v0 corresponds to the negative of the highest root. In this paper, we adopt the root systems Φ An , ΦBn , ΦCn , ΦDn , given in Table 2, as the con-crete realizations for the classical root systems of type A n, Bn, Cn, Dn, respectively. # 4 Combinatorial structures arising from root systems 

In this section, we introduce the combinatorial structures arising from root systems, which serve as the underlying discrete domains for discrete convex analysis. Section 4.1 defines root lattices 

and coroot lattices . Section 4.2 details spherical and Euclidean Coxeter complexes , including their spherical expressions, geometric realizations, (positively homogeneous) Lov´ asz extensions, and the properties of their convex subcomplexes. Let Φ be a root system. In this section, for notational simplicity, we assume that Φ is essential, i.e., V ∗ = span Φ; if Φ is not essential, in the following argument, we replace V and 

V ∗ with V / (span Φ) ⊥ and span Φ, respectively; recall Section 2.2. Let n := dim span Φ, which coincides with dim V ∗ = dim V by the assumption.

4.2 Spherical and Euclidean Coxeter complexes 

This subsection is devoted to introducing spherical Coxeter complexes and Euclidean Coxeter complexes . These are special cases of a more general concept called Coxeter complexes . In this paper, we only introduce spherical/Euclidean Coxeter complexes and we adapt the arguments for general Coxeter complexes to these special cases. See [38, 1, 27] for details. 

4.2.1 Definitions 

Let Φ be a root system. For α ∈ Φ and k ∈ Z, we define a hyperplane H0 

> α,k

in V by 

H0 

> α,k

:= {x ∈ V | ⟨ α, x ⟩ = k},

and open half-spaces H+ 

> α,k

and H− 

> α,k

by 

H+ 

> α,k

:= {x ∈ V | ⟨ α, x ⟩ > k }, H− 

> α,k

:= {x ∈ V | ⟨ α, x ⟩ < k }.

Let Hlin (Φ) and Haff (Φ) be the hyperplane arrangements defined by 

Hlin (Φ) := {H0 

> α, 0

| α ∈ Φ}, Haff (Φ) := {H0 

> α,k

| α ∈ Φ, k ∈ Z},

21 respectively. Note that each hyperplane H in Hlin (Φ) (resp. in Haff (Φ)) corresponds to exactly two indices α, −α in Φ (resp. ( α, k ), (−α, −k) in Φ × Z). A nonempty subset A ⊆ V is called an (open) cell with respect to Hlin (Φ) if there is a sequence 

σ(A) = ( σα(A)) α∈Φ ∈ { +, 0, −} Φ such that

,

> (α,k )∈Φ×Z

.

In both cases, the sequence σ(A) corresponding to A is uniquely determined; we call σ(A) the 

sign sequence of A.We are ready to define spherical and Euclidean Coxeter complexes: Examples are given in Tables 4 and 5. The spherical Coxeter complex of type Φ, denoted by Σ sp (Φ), is the collection of all open cells with respect to Hlin (Φ), endowed with the facial relation ⪯ as: A ⪯ B if and only if cl A ⊆ cl B.In the spherical Coxeter complex Σ sp (Φ), the least element ⊥ is {0}. If Φ is a root system of type A, B, C, or D, then we refer to Σ sp (Φ) as the spherical Coxeter complex of type A, B, C, or D, respectively. If Φ 1 ≲ Φ2, then we have Hlin (Φ 1) ⊆ H lin (Φ 2), which implies that Σ sp (Φ 1) is a coarse simplicial complex of Σ sp (Φ 2) in the sense that, for any cell A2 ∈ Σsp (Φ 2), there exists a unique cell A1 ∈ Σsp (Φ 1) such that A1 ⊇ A2. In particular, if two root systems Φ 1 and Φ 2 are equivalent, then Σ sp (Φ 1) = Σ sp (Φ 2). Hence, the spherical Coxeter complexes of type B n and of type C n are the same; see Table 4. In considering the Euclidean Coxeter complex, we assume that Φ is irreducible. The Euclidean Coxeter complex of type Φ, denoted by Σ Euc (Φ), is the collection of all open cells with respect to Haff (Φ) with the empty set ∅, endowed with the facial relation ⪯ as: A ⪯ B if and only if cl A ⊆ cl B. In the Euclidean Coxeter complex Σ Euc (Φ), the least element ⊥ is ∅. We define the sign sequence of the least element ∅ as (0) (α,k )∈Φ×Z. If Φ is a root system of type A, B, C, or D, then we refer to Σ Euc (Φ) as the Euclidean Coxeter complex of type A, B, C, or D, respectively. Unlike spherical Coxeter complexes, even if two irreducible root systems Φ 1 and Φ2 are equivalent, the corresponding Euclidean Coxeter complexes Σ Euc (Φ 1) and Σ Euc (Φ 2) are different in general (see Table 4 again). Both Σ sp (Φ) and Σ Euc (Φ) are simplicial complexes, or more precisely, colorable chamber complexes [1, Theorem 3.5], whose ranks are n and n + 1, respectively. The following provides the one-to-one correspondence between the chambers and the simple systems (with the members of the coroot lattice in the case of a Euclidean Coxeter complex): 

Lemma 4.1 ([27, Proposition 8.21] and [1, Chapter 10.1.8]) . (1) For each chamber C of Σsp (Φ) ,there exists a unique simple system ∆ such that 

C = {x ∈ V | ⟨ α, x ⟩ > 0 ( ∀α ∈ ∆) }. (4.3) 

Conversely, for each simple system ∆, the set C defined as (4.3) is a chamber of Σsp (Φ) .

(2) For each chamber C of ΣEuc (Φ) , there uniquely exist a simple system ∆ and x0 ∈ L ∨(Φ) 

such that 

C = x0 + {x ∈ V | ⟨ α0, x ⟩ > −1 and ⟨α, x ⟩ > 0 ( ∀α ∈ ∆) }, (4.4) 22 where α0 is the negative of the highest root with respect to the simple system ∆. Conversely, for each simple system ∆ and x0 ∈ L ∨(Φ) , the set C defined as (4.4) is a chamber of 

ΣEuc (Φ) .

The above lemma (Lemma 4.1) also says that each chamber C of Σ sp (Φ) (resp. Σ Euc (Φ)) can be characterized by the inequalities indexed by the simple system ∆ (resp. the extended simple system ˜∆ = ∆ ∪ { α0}) corresponding to C. By using the integral vector ˜d ∈ Z ˜∆ defined by ˜d(α) := −1 if α = α0 and ˜d(α) := 0 if α ∈ ∆, the formulation (4.4) of a chamber C of Σ Euc (Φ) is representable as 

C = x0 + {x ∈ V | ⟨ α, x ⟩ > ˜d(α) ( ∀α ∈ ˜∆) }.

Fix an arbitrary chamber C ∈ Σsp (Φ) and let ∆ be the simple system corresponding to C.Then, for each vertex x ∈ V (C), there exists a unique root α ∈ ∆ such that ⟨α, x ⟩ > 0. This enables us to define the bijection τC : V(C) → D(Φ) by τC (x) := τ∆(α), where α is the unique root in ∆ satisfying ⟨α, x ⟩ > 0. We define the canonical type function τΣsp (Φ) : V(Σ sp (Φ)) →

D(Φ) of Σ sp (Φ) by extending the bijection τC (see the last paragraph of Section 2.1). We can similarly define the canonical type function τΣEuc (Φ) : V(Σ Euc (Φ)) → ˜D(Φ) of Σ Euc (Φ). Note that the canonical type function τΣsp (Φ) (resp. τΣEuc (Φ) ) is independent of the choice of a chamber 

C ∈ Σsp (Φ) (resp. C ∈ ΣEuc (Φ)). The subscripts of τΣsp (Φ) and τΣEuc (Φ) may be omitted if it is clear from the context. For a simplex A ∈ Σsp (Φ) and the simple system ∆ corresponding to a chamber C ∈

St Σsp (Φ) (A), i.e., a chamber C of Σ sp (Φ) with C ⪰ A, we define ∆|A := ∆ |τ (A), ∆|−A := ∆ |−τ (A).

Similarly, for a simplex A ∈ ΣEuc (Φ) and the extended simple system ˜∆ corresponding to a chamber C ∈ St ΣEuc (Φ) (A), we define ˜∆|A := ˜∆|τ (A), ˜∆|−A := ˜∆|−τ (A).

Then, by a general theory of Coxeter complexes and Lemma 4.1, each simplex A of Σ sp (Φ) or ΣEuc (Φ) admits an easy representation as follows.

For Σ = Σ sp (Φ) or Σ = Σ Euc (Φ), the mark of a vertex x ∈ V (Σ), denoted by aΣ(x), is defined as the mark of its type τΣ(x) (see the last paragraph of Section 3.2 for the definition of the mark of the types); we refer to aΣ : V(Σ) → Z+ as the mark function of Σ. The subscript Σ sp (Φ) of 

aΣsp (Φ) (resp. Σ Euc (Φ) of aΣEuc (Φ) ) may be omitted if it is clear from the context. We present explicit descriptions of the chambers of the spherical and Euclidean Coxeter complexes associated with the classical root systems. As we can see in Corollary 4.2 (and Table 4), while the closure of each simplex in Σ Euc (Φ) is a geometric simplex in V , that of each simplex in Σ sp (Φ) is a simplicial cone in V . In particular, while a vertex in Σ Euc (Φ) is a point in V , that in Σ sp (Φ) is an open half-line in V . 4.2.2 Spherical expressions of spherical Coxeter complexes 

Let Φ be a root system. Take an arbitrary point xℓ ∈ ℓ for each vertex ℓ ∈ V (Σ sp (Φ)), and define A◦ as the relative interior of conv {xℓ | ℓ ∈ V (A)} for each cell A ∈ Σsp (Φ) \ { 0}. Then, the spherical expression Σ◦ of Σ sp (Φ) generated from {xℓ | ℓ ∈ V (Σ sp (Φ)) } is the poset defined by Σ◦ := ( {A◦ | A ∈ Σsp (Φ) \ { 0}} ∪ {∅} , ⪯),

where ∅ ⪯ A◦ for any A◦, and A◦ ⪯ B◦ if cl A◦ ⊆ cl B◦. The correspondence A 7 → A◦ and 

{0} 7 → ∅ forms an isomorphism between Σ sp (Φ) and its spherical expression as a poset, and the inverse map is given by A◦ 7 → cone A◦. Hence, a spherical expression is also a Coxeter complex that admits the canonical type function xℓ 7 → τ (ℓ) and the mark function xℓ 7 → a(ℓ), which are denoted by τΣ◦ and aΣ◦ , respectively. Similarly, for A◦ ∈ Σ◦, its link complex Lk Σ◦ (A◦) is naturally isomorphic to Lk Σsp (Φ) (A), which enables us to introduce the canonical type function and the mark function with respect to Lk Σ◦ (A◦) as well. Suppose further that Φ = Φ 1⊕Φ2⊕· · ·⊕ Φk in which Φ 1, Φ2, . . . , Φk are irreducible components of Φ. Take any vertex ℓ ∈ V (Σ sp (Φ)). Let C be a chamber with ℓ ⪯ C, i.e., ℓ ∈ V (C), and ∆ = ∆ 1 ∪ · · · ∪ ∆k the simple system corresponding to C in which ∆ 1, ∆2, . . . , ∆k are simple systems of the irreducible component Φ 1, Φ2, . . . , Φk of Φ, respectively. C with ℓ ⪯ C. We refer to x⋆ℓ as the standard point of ℓ. The standard spherical expression of Σsp (Φ), denoted by Σ ⋆

4.2.3 Geometric realization and (positively homogeneous) Lov´ asz extension 

Let Σ be a spherical Coxeter complex, a Coxeter complex, or a spherical expression of the spherical Coxeter complex. For a subset Σ ′ of Σ, its geometric realization |Σ′| is defined by 

|Σ′| := [

> A∈Σ′

A, 

which is a subset of V .Suppose that Σ is a spherical or Euclidean Coxeter complex. Then, we have |Σ| = V . Since any two distinct simplices are disjoint, Σ forms a partition of V . Hence, for each x ∈ | Σ| = V ,there exists a unique cell in Σ that contains x, which is denoted by Ax. For a vertex subset 

D ⊆ V (Σ), its Lov´ asz extension D is defined as the geometric realization |Σ[ D]| of the subcomplex of Σ induced by D, i.e., 

D := |Σ[ D]| = [{A ∈ Σ | V (A) ⊆ D},

28 which is also a subset of V . Note that a point x ∈ V belongs to D if and only if V(Ax) ⊆ D.We particularly focus on the case where Σ is a Euclidean Coxeter complex Σ Euc := Σ Euc (Φ). Since Ax is a geometric simplex in V that contains x in its relative interior, each point x ∈ V

is uniquely representable as the convex combination of the vertices in V(Ax); let λx ∈ ΛV(Σ Euc )

In the following arguments of this subsection, ( H, Σ) denotes ( Hlin (Φ) , Σsp (Φ)) or ( Haff (Φ) , ΣEuc (Φ)); this notation is used in the arguments that can be applied to both spherical and Euclidean Cox-eter complexes. b ∈ B, and sufficiently small ε > 0. It is known ([1, Corollary 3.110]) that Σ is a semigroup under the binary operation ( A, B ) 7 → AB , i.e., for all A, B, C ∈ Σ, we have ( AB )C = A(BC ). A subcomplex Σ ′ of Σ is said to be convex if Σ ′ is a subsemigroup of Σ, i.e., AB ∈ Σ′ for any cells A, B ∈ Σ′. The following lemma (Lemma 4.9) says that the convexity of a subcomplex Σ ′

of Σ can be characterized as the representability of Σ ′ as the intersection of closed half-spaces arising from hyperplanes in H, and as the convexity of the geometric realization |Σ′| in V .

= 12 · #{i ∈ I | { σi(A), σ i(B)} = {+, −}} . (4.15) We introduce a partial order ≺ on {+, 0, −} as: + ≻ 0 ≺ − , and + and − are incomparable. This partial order ≺ can be naturally extended to that on the sign sequences: σ(A) ⪯ σ(B) if and only if σi(A) ⪯ σi(B) for all i ∈ I. We say that σ(A) and σ(B) are compatible if there is no 

i ∈ I such that {σi(A), σ i(B)} = {+, −} .We summarize the basic properties of the product and the distance. 

Lemma 4.12. Let A, A 1, . . . , A k, B be cells in Σ.

(1) A ⪯ B if and only if σ(A) ⪯ σ(B).

(2) ABA = AB .

(3) A1, . . . , A k are joinable if and only if σ(A1), . . . , σ (Ak) are mutually compatible. In addi-tion, if A1, . . . , A k are joinable, its join is the product A1 · · · Ak of A1, . . . , A k.

(4) If A1 and A2 are joinable, then d(A1A2, B ) ≥ d(A1, A 2B).

(5) If A1 ⪯ A2, then d(A1, B ) ≤ d(A2, B ).
