Hence, σ(α,k )(x) ⪯ σ(α,k )(Az ) holds, which implies x ⪯ Az by the assertion (1). 

# 5 Discrete convexity in Euclidean spaces 

This section formulates discrete convexity in Euclidean spaces to provide a geometric foundation for the discrete theory. Section 5.1 defines L- and M-convex cones. Sections 5.2 and 5.3 for-mulate L- and M-convex polyhedra , L- and M-sublinear functions , and their integrality. Finally, Section 5.4 introduces locally polyhedral L- and M-convex functions and reveals the conjugacy between them. Throughout Sections 5–7, we assume that Φ is a root system such that its irreducible com-ponents are of classical types. Note that any subsystem of a classical root system satisfies this assumption (see Lemma 6.17 (2)). Let Σ sp := Σ sp (Φ), Σ ⋆ 

> sp

:= Σ ⋆

> sp

(Φ), and Σ Euc := Σ Euc (Φ) denote the spherical Coxeter complex, the standard spherical expression of the spherical Coxeter complex, and Euclidean Coxeter complexes of type Φ, respectively; in considering the Euclidean Coxeter complex, we assume that Φ is irreducible. Let V := V(Σ Euc ) denote the vertex set of ΣEuc , and L := L(Φ) the root lattice of Φ. All notions involving L- or M-convexity are defined relative to the fixed root system Φ. Accordingly, the full names of these notions include the qualifier “of type Φ.” For notational simplicity, we omit this qualifier whenever the underlying root system is clear from the context. 

Remark 5.1. The above assumption of classical type is imposed so that all statements in Sections 5–7 can be presented under a common hypothesis. Most of the definitions and arguments in these sections do not use the classification of irreducible root systems. The definitions of L-and M-convex cones, polyhedra, sublinear functions, locally polyhedral convex functions, and the corresponding discrete notions can be formulated for arbitrary irreducible root systems. The arguments that depend on the classical types are essentially confined to Proposition 5.7, which gives an explicit type-by-type characterization of tight distance functions, and Propo-sition 6.8. In particular, the results on L-convexity in Proposition 6.2 and Theorems 6.5, 7.1 and 7.2 and the discrete conjugacy theorem (Theorem 6.11) remain valid for arbitrary irreducible root systems, including the exceptional types. By contrast, the M-convex local optimality and intrinsic characterizations that use Proposition 6.8 are established here under the classical-type assumption. ■

32 5.1 L- and M-convex cones 

A polyhedral cone K ⊆ V is said to be L-convex of type Φ if it admits an outer-representation 

K = {x ∈ V | ⟨ α, x ⟩ ≤ 0 ( ∀α ∈ Φ′)} (5.1) for some Φ ′ ⊆ Φ. Similarly, a polyhedral cone K ⊆ V ∗ is said to be M-convex of type Φ if it admits an inner-representation 

K = cone Φ ′

for some Φ ′ ⊆ Φ. We note that, for a root system Φ − with Φ − ≾ Φ, an L-convex (resp. M-convex) cone of type Φ − is also L-convex (resp. M-convex) of type Φ. The following one-to-one correspondence between the L- and M-convex cones is a direct consequence of Lemma 2.6. 

Theorem 5.2. The polar cone of an L-convex cone is an M-convex cone, and vice versa. 

Lemma 4.9 (particularly, the inequality expression (4.13)) and the definition of L-convex cones (5.1) immediately imply that the L-convex cones are exactly the geometric realizations of nonempty convex subcomplexes of Σ sp . For later reference, we summarize this characterization of L-convex cones. 

Lemma 5.3. A set K ⊆ V is an L-convex cone if and only if it is the geometric realization of some nonempty convex subcomplex of Σsp .

We note that L-convex cones and M-convex cones are exactly the same as Coxeter cones [64] and Coxeter root cones [2], respectively. 

5.2 L-convex polyhedra and M-sublinear functions 

We refer to a function γ : Φ → R ∪ { +∞} as a distance function of type Φ. For consistency with existing notation in DCA [48, Chapter 5.2], we denote the polyhedron S(γ) by D(γ), i.e., 

D(γ) := S(γ) = {x ∈ V | ⟨ α, x ⟩ ≤ γ(α) ( ∀α ∈ Φ) }.

Similarly, we denote S(cone γ) by D(cone γ); recall that D(cone γ) = D(γ) (Lemma 2.9). We say that a distance function γ : Φ → R ∪ { +∞} is valid if D(γ) is a nonempty polyhedron, or equivalently, cone γ is a proper polyhedral sublinear function. A valid distance function γ : Φ →

R ∪ { +∞} is said to be integral if there is an integer-valued distance function γ′ : Φ → Z ∪ { +∞} 

such that D(γ) = D(γ′), or equivalently, cone γ = cone γ′.A set P ⊆ V is called an L-convex polyhedron of type Φ if P = D(γ) for some valid distance function γ. In addition, an L-convex polyhedron P is integral if P = D(γ) for some integral valid distance function γ. We denote by L0[R] (resp. L0[Z|R]) the family of L-convex polyhedra (resp. integral L-convex polyhedra). We note that an L-convex cone is an integral L-convex polyhedron, since it is representable as D(γ) for some γ : Φ → { 0, +∞} .A function σ : V ∗ → R ∪ { +∞} is called an M-sublinear function of type Φ if σ = cone γ

for some valid distance function γ. In addition, an M-sublinear function is said to be integral 

if σ = cone γ for some integral valid distance function γ. We denote by 0M[R → R] (resp. 

> 0

M[R → Z|R]) the family of M-sublinear functions (resp. integral M-sublinear functions). When we emphasize the integrality of an L-convex polyhedron (resp. an M-sublinear function) of type Φ, we specifically use the term Φ -integral .33 For a root system Φ − with Φ − ≾ Φ, although an L-convex polyhedron (resp. an M-sublinear function) of type Φ − is L-convex (resp. M-sublinear) of type Φ, a Φ −-integral L-convex polyhe-dron (resp. a Φ −-integral M-sublinear function) is not necessarily Φ-integral in general. We immediately obtain the following one-to-one correspondence between L-convex polyhedra and M-sublinear functions from Lemma 2.9 and the definitions of L-convex polyhedra and M-sublinear functions. 

Theorem 5.4. The support function σ[P ] of an (integral) L-convex polyhedron P is an (integral) M-sublinear function, and the set D(σ) arising from an (integral) M-sublinear function σ is an (integral) L-convex polyhedron. 

Lemma 4.9 (particularly, the inequality expression (4.14)) and the definition of integral L-convex polyhedra immediately imply that, if Φ is an irreducible root system, then the integral L-convex polyhedra are exactly the geometric realizations of nonempty convex subcomplexes of Σ Euc . For later reference, we explicitly describe this characterization of integral L-convex polyhedra. 

Lemma 5.5. Suppose that Φ is irreducible. Then, a set P ⊆ V is an integral L-convex polyhedron if and only if it is the geometric realization of some nonempty convex subcomplex of ΣEuc .

The following lemma states that the M-sublinearity of a polyhedral sublinear function σ can be characterized as the M-convexity of the polyhedral cones in Π( σ), which is the counterpart of the definition of an L-sublinear function; see Section 5.3 below. 

Lemma 5.6. A proper polyhedral sublinear function σ : V ∗ → R ∪ { +∞} is M-sublinear if and only if every member of Π( σ) is an M-convex cone. Proof. (Only-if part). Since σ is M-sublinear, there is a valid γ : Φ → R ∪ { +∞} such that 

σ = cone γ. Take any K ∈ Π( σ). Then, for some x ∈ V , we have K = argmin( σ − x) = argmin(cone γ − x). Since K is nonempty, we have γ(α) − ⟨ α, x ⟩ ≥ 0 for any α ∈ dom γ. Thus, 

K = cone {α ∈ Φ | γ(α) = ⟨α, x ⟩} , which implies that K is an M-convex cone. (If part). Since every member of Π( σ) is an M-convex cone, for each K ∈ Π( σ), there is ΦK ⊆ Φ such that K = cone Φ K . Let γ : Φ → R ∪ { +∞} be the restriction of σ to Φ, i.e., 

γ(α) := σ(α) for α ∈ Φ. Then, for K ∈ Π( σ) and p ∈ K, we have σ(p) = P 

> α∈ΦK

μ(α)γ(α) ≥

(cone γ)( p) by the definition of cone γ (2.3), where μ ∈ MΦK satisfying P 

> α∈ΦK

μ(α)α = p.Thus, we obtain σ ≥ cone γ. On the other hand, since σ is a proper sublinear function satisfying 

σ(α) = γ(α) for α ∈ Φ and cone γ is the pointwise maximum proper sublinear function satisfying (cone γ)( α) ≤ γ(α) for all α ∈ Φ, we have σ ≤ cone γ. This implies that σ = cone γ, i.e., σ is M-sublinear. 

5.2.1 Tight distance functions 

A distance function γ : Φ → R ∪ { +∞} is said to be tight if γ is the restriction of an M-sublinear function to Φ, i.e., γ is a valid distance function with γ = (cone γ)|Φ. Moreover, a tight distance function is said to be integral if it is the restriction of an integral M-sublinear function. Let ˇT [R]denote the family of all valid distance functions, and T [R] (resp. T [Z]) the family of tight (resp. integral tight) distance functions. See the commutative diagram (6.1) below for the relations among L0[R], L0[Z|R], 0M[R → R], 0M[R → Z|R], ˇT [R], T [R], and T [Z]. We conclude Section 5.2.1 with a “triangle-inequality-like” characterization of tight distance functions for each type of root system; note that the condition for type A is exactly the same as that presented in [48, Sections 5.2 and 5.6]. Unlike the preceding results, the following explicit characterization uses the coordinate descriptions of the classical root systems. The definition 34 of tight distance functions and the general correspondences developed above do not require this restriction. Here, for notational simplicity, we consider 2Φ Cn instead of Φ Cn in the case of Type C. 

Proposition 5.7. A distance function γ : Φ → R ∪ { +∞} is tight if and only if γ(α)+ γ(−α) ≥ 0

holds for all α ∈ Φ and the following conditions hold for each case of Φ = Φ An (Type A), Φ = Φ Bn

(Type B), Φ = 2Φ Cn (Type C), and Φ = Φ Dn (Type D): 

(Type A) γ(α1) + γ(α2) ≥ γ(α1 + α2) for any distinct α1, α 2 ∈ Φ with α1 + α2 ∈ Φ, which is equivalent to γ(ei − ej ) + γ(ej − ek) ≥ γ(ei − ek) for any distinct i, j, k ∈ [n + 1] .

(Type B) γ(α1) + γ(α2) ≥ γ(α1 + α2) for any distinct α1, α 2 ∈ Φ with α1 + α2 ∈ Φ, and 

γ(α1) + γ(α2) ≥ 2γ(( α1 + α2)/2) for any distinct α1, α 2 ∈ Φ with (α1 + α2)/2 ∈ Φ, which are equivalent to 

• γ(σiei + σj ej ) + γ(−σj ej + σkek) ≥ γ(σiei + σkek),

• γ(σiei) + γ(σj ej ) ≥ γ(σiei + σj ej ),

• γ(σiei) + γ(−σiei + σj ej ) ≥ γ(σj ej ), and 

• γ(σiei + σj ej ) + γ(σiei − σj ej ) ≥ 2γ(σiei)

for any distinct i, j, k ∈ [n] and any σi, σ j , σ k ∈ {± 1} in the first bullet, and for any distinct 

i, j ∈ [n] and any σi, σ j ∈ {± 1} in the other bullets. 

(Type C) γ(α1) + γ(α2) ≥ γ(α1 + α2) for any distinct α1, α 2 ∈ Φ with α1 + α2 ∈ Φ, and 

γ(α1) + γ(α2) ≥ 2γ(( α1 + α2)/2) for any distinct α1, α 2 ∈ Φ with (α1 + α2)/2 ∈ Φ, which are equivalent to 

• γ(σiei + σj ej ) + γ(−σj ej + σkek) ≥ γ(σiei + σkek),

• γ(σiei + σj ej ) + γ(σiei − σj ej ) ≥ γ(2 σiei),

• γ(2 σiei) + γ(−σiei + σj ej ) ≥ γ(σiei + σj ej ), and 

• γ(2 σiei) + γ(2 σj ej ) ≥ 2γ(σiei + σj ej )

for any distinct i, j, k ∈ [n] and any σi, σ j , σ k ∈ {± 1} in the first bullet, and for any distinct 

i, j ∈ [n] and any σi, σ j ∈ {± 1} in the other bullets. 

(Type D) γ(α1) + γ(α2) ≥ γ(α1 + α2) for any distinct α1, α 2 ∈ Φ with α1 + α2 ∈ Φ, and 

γ(α1)+ γ(α2)+ γ(α3)+ γ(α4) ≥ 2γ(( α1 +α2 +α3 +α4)/2) for any distinct α1, α 2, α 3, α 4 ∈ Φ

with (α1 + α2 + α3 + α4)/2 ∈ Φ, which are equivalent to 

• γ(σiei + σj ej ) + γ(−σj ej + σkek) ≥ γ(σiei + σkek) and 

• γ(σiei + σi′ ei′ ) + γ(σiei − σi′ ei′ ) + γ(σj ej + σj′ ej′ ) + γ(σj ej − σj′ ej′ ) ≥ 2γ(σiei + σj ej )

for any distinct i, j, k ∈ [n] and any σi, σ j , σ k ∈ {± 1} in the first bullet, and for any distinct 

i, i ′, j, j ′ ∈ [n] and any σi, σ i′ , σ j , σ j′ ∈ {± 1} in the second bullet. 

The above proposition is of independent interest, but lies outside the main line of development and is not used in the proofs of our other results. We therefore defer its proof to Section A.1. 35 5.2.2 Basic properties of L-convex polyhedra 

We here summarize basic properties of L-convex polyhedra. 

Lemma 5.8. Every nonempty face of an (integral) L-convex polyhedron is an (integral) L-convex polyhedron. Proof. Let P be an L-convex polyhedron and suppose that P admits an outer-representation 

P = D(γ) for a distance function γ : Φ → R ∪ { +∞} . Then, by a well-known fact (see e.g., [61, Chapter 8.3]) on the faces of a polyhedron, for any nonempty face F of P , there is a subset Φ′ ⊆ dom γ such that 

F = {x ∈ V | ⟨ α, x ⟩ = γ(α) ( ∀α ∈ Φ′), ⟨α, x ⟩ ≤ γ(α) ( ∀α ∈ Φ \ Φ′)}.

Hence, F is representable as F = D(γ′) for some distance function γ′, implying that F is an L-convex polyhedron. In particular, if P is integral, then we can take γ′ as integer-valued. Therefore, F is also integral. The definition of L-convex polyhedra immediately implies the following: 

Lemma 5.9. The intersection of arbitrarily many (integral) L-convex polyhedra is an (integral) L-convex polyhedron if it is nonempty. Proof. Let {Pλ}λ∈Λ be a family of L-convex polyhedra, where Λ is an arbitrary index set, and 

P := T 

> λ∈Λ

Pλ. Suppose that P is nonempty. For each λ ∈ Λ, there is a distance function 

γλ : Φ → R ∪ { +∞} such that Pλ = D(γλ). Then, we have P = T 

> λ∈Λ

D(γλ) = D(γ), where 

γ := inf λ∈Λ γλ. Since P is nonempty, γ never takes −∞ and γ is valid. Thus, P is an L-convex polyhedron. If each Pλ is integral, then we can take γλ to be integer-valued for each λ ∈ Λ. Hence, 

γ := inf λ∈Λ γλ is also integer-valued, which implies that P is an integral L-convex polyhedron. The following provides the characterization of L-convex polyhedra as the L-convexity of its tangent cone, which is the counterpart of the definition of M-convex polyhedra (see Section 5.3 below). 

Lemma 5.10. A nonempty closed convex set C is an L-convex polyhedron if and only if, for any x ∈ C, the tangent cone Tx(C) of C at x is an L-convex cone. Proof. (Only-if part). Since C is an L-convex polyhedron, there exists a valid function γ : Φ →

R ∪ { +∞} such that C = D(γ). Take any x ∈ C. It follows from [4, Proposition 3.5.2] that the tangent cone T x(C) is of the form {y ∈ V | ⟨ α, y ⟩ ≤ 0 ( ∀α ∈ Φ′)}, where Φ ′ := {α ∈

dom γ | ⟨ α, x ⟩ = γ(α)}. Thus, T x(C) is an L-convex cone. (If part). For each x ∈ C, the set x + T x(C) forms an L-convex polyhedron, since T x(C)is an L-convex cone. By Lemma 2.1, we have C = T 

> x∈C

(x + T x(C)). Thus, C is an L-convex polyhedron by Lemma 5.9. 

Lemma 5.11. The union of arbitrarily many integral L-convex polyhedra is closed. Proof. Let {D(γλ)}λ∈Λ be an arbitrary family of integral L-convex polyhedra, where γλ : Φ → Z

is an integer-valued distance function for each λ ∈ Λ, and L := S 

> λ∈Λ

D(γλ). Take any x ∈ V \ L.Then, for all λ ∈ Λ, there is a root αλ ∈ Φ such that γλ(αλ) ∈ Z and ⟨αλ, x ⟩ > γ λ(αλ). Since 

{αλ | λ ∈ Λ} ⊆ Φ is a finite set, we have # {⟨ αλ, x ⟩ | λ ∈ Λ} < +∞. By γλ(αλ) ∈ Z, there is 

δ > 0 such that ⟨αλ, x ⟩ − δ > γ λ(αλ) for any λ ∈ Λ. Therefore, there is a sufficiently small ε > 0such that, for all d ∈ V with ∥d∥ < ε and λ ∈ Λ, we have ⟨αλ, x + d⟩ > γ λ(αλ). This implies that the open ball B(x; ε) centered at x with radius ε is contained in V \ L. Hence, L is closed. 36 5.3 M-convex polyhedra and L-sublinear functions 

A nonempty set P ⊆ V ∗ is called an M-convex polyhedron of type Φ if it is a closed convex set such that, for any p ∈ P , the tangent cone T p(P ) of P at p is an M-convex cone (cf. Lemma 5.10). An M-convex polyhedron P is actually a polyhedron. Indeed, since the number of M-convex cones of type Φ is bounded by that of subsets of Φ, particularly, a finite value, the set {Tp(P ) | p ∈ P } is a finite set, which implies that P is a polyhedron by Lemma 2.8. An M-convex polyhedron P is said to be integral if it is L-integral, i.e., P = conv( P ∩ L ). We note that an M-convex cone is an integral M-convex polyhedron. Indeed, for an M-convex cone 

K, which is representable as K = cone Φ ′ for some Φ ′ ⊆ Φ, and p = Pki=1 μiαi ∈ K with 

α1, α 2, . . . , α k ∈ Φ′ and μ1, μ 2, . . . , μ k > 0, we have T p(K) = cone(Φ ′ ∪ {− α1, −α2, . . . , −αk}); this implies that K is an M-convex polyhedron. Moreover, since K = conv {0} + cone Φ ′, it is L-integral by Lemma 2.12. We denote by M0[R] (resp. M0[Z|R]) the family of M-convex polyhedra (resp. integral M-convex polyhedra). A polyhedral sublinear function σ : V → R ∪ { +∞} is called an L-sublinear function of type 

Φ if every member of Π( σ) is an L-convex cone (cf. Lemma 5.6). An L-sublinear function σ is said to be integral if there is a finite subset B ⊆ L such that σ is representable as 

σ(x) = max {⟨ p, x ⟩ | p ∈ B}

for x ∈ dom σ. In other words, a polyhedral sublinear function σ : V → R ∪ { +∞} is an integral L-sublinear function if and only if every member of Π( σ) is an L-convex cone and, for any maximal K ∈ Π( σ), there is p ∈ L such that K = argmin( σ − p). We denote by 0L[R → R](resp. 0L[R → Z|R]) the family of L-sublinear functions (resp. integral L-sublinear functions). When we emphasize the integrality of an M-convex polyhedron (resp. an L-sublinear function) of type Φ, we specifically use the term Φ -integral .For a root system Φ − with Φ − ≾ Φ, although an M-convex polyhedron (resp. an L-sublinear function) of type Φ − is M-convex (resp. L-sublinear) of type Φ, a Φ −-integral M-convex polyhe-dron (resp. a Φ −-integral L-sublinear function) is not necessarily Φ-integral. As in the case of L-convex polyhedra and M-sublinear functions (Theorem 5.4), we establish the one-to-one correspondence between the (integral) M-convex polyhedra and the (integral) L-sublinear functions as follows. Here, for an L-sublinear function σ : V → R ∪ { +∞} , we denote the polyhedron S(σ) by B(σ) (cf. [48, Chapter 4.3]). 

Theorem 5.12. The support function σ[P ] of an (integral) M-convex polyhedron P is an (in-tegral) L-sublinear function, and the set B(σ) arising from an L-sublinear function σ is an (integral) M-convex polyhedron. 

The following lemma is an easy observation on L-sublinear functions, which is used in the proof of Theorem 5.12. 

Lemma 5.13. The effective domain of an L-sublinear function is an L-convex cone. Proof. Suppose that σ : V → R ∪ { +∞} is an L-sublinear function. Then, we have dom σ =

|Π( σ)| = S 

> K∈Π( σ)

K. Since each K ∈ Π( σ) is an L-convex cone, it is the geometric realization of a convex subcomplex Σ K of Σ sp by Lemma 5.3. Then, Σ ′ := S 

> K∈Π( σ)

ΣK is also a convex subcomplex of Σ sp by Lemma 4.9, since dom σ = S 

> K∈Π( σ)

|ΣK | = |Σ′| is convex. Thus, dom σ is the geometric realization of a convex subcomplex of Σ sp , which implies that dom σ is an L-convex cone by Lemma 5.3. We are ready to show Theorem 5.12. 37 Proof of Theorem 5.12. If P is an M-convex polyhedron, then we have Π( σ[P ]) = {(T p(P )) ∗ | p ∈

P } by Lemma 2.10. Since T p(P ) is an M-convex cone for any p ∈ P by the definition, its po-lar cone (T p(P )) ∗ is an L-convex cone by Theorem 5.2. Thus, Π( σ[P ]) consists of L-convex cones, which implies that σ[P ] is an L-sublinear function. Conversely, if σ is an L-sublinear function, then B(σ) is a nonempty polyhedron. By Lemma 2.10, for any p ∈ B(σ) we have Tp(B(σ)) = (argmin( σ − p)) ∗. Since argmin( σ − p) ∈ Π( σ) is an L-convex cone, its polar cone Tp(B(σ)) is an M-convex cone by Theorem 5.2. Thus, B(σ) is an M-convex polyhedron. By the above correspondence, the recession cone of an M-convex polyhedron is an M-convex cone. Indeed, for an M-convex polyhedron P and its corresponding L-sublinear function σ, the recession cone of P is (dom σ)∗ by Lemma 2.9, which is an M-convex cone by Theorem 5.2 and Lemma 5.13. We then see that the one-to-one correspondence between M-convex polyhedra and L-sublinear functions preserves integrality. If P is an integral M-convex polyhedron, then P admits an inner-representation P = conv B + cone Φ ′ for some finite sets B ⊆ L and Φ ′ ⊆ Φ ⊆ L by Lemma 2.12. Here, we have already seen that σ[P ] is L-sublinear. By Lemma 2.9, we obtain σ[P ]( x) = max {⟨ p, x ⟩ | p ∈ B} for x ∈ dom( σ[P ]). Thus, σ[P ] is integral. Conversely, if σ is an integral L-sublinear function, then there is a finite set B ⊆ L such that σ(x) = max {⟨ p, x ⟩ | p ∈ B} for 

x ∈ dom σ. Here, we have already seen that B(σ) is an M-convex polyhedron. By Lemma 2.9, we obtain B(σ) = conv B + cone Φ ′, which implies that B(σ) is integral by Lemma 2.12. 

5.3.1 (Spherical) submodular functions 

Fix an arbitrary spherical expression Σ ◦ of Σ sp . A function ρ : V(Σ ◦) → R ∪ { +∞} is said to be 

spherical submodular of type Φ if ρ is the restriction of some L-sublinear function σ to V(Σ ◦). In addition, a spherical submodular function ρ is said to be integral if there is an integral L-sublinear function σ of type Φ such that ρ is the restriction of σ to V(Σ ◦). For consistency with existing notation in DCA [48, Chapter 4.3], for a spherical submodular function ρ : V(Σ ◦) → R ∪ { +∞} ,we denote the polyhedron S(ρ) by B(ρ), i.e., 

B(ρ) := S(ρ) = {p ∈ V ∗ | ⟨ p, x ⟩ ≤ ρ(x) ( ∀x ∈ dom ρ)}.

We particularly refer to B(ρ) as the base polyhedron of ρ.The following proposition states that the spherical submodular functions play a role as the tight distance functions in Section 5.2 and that they can be characterized as the convexity of its positively homogeneous Lov´ asz extension. 

Proposition 5.14. Let Σ◦ be a spherical expression of Σsp and ρ : V(Σ ◦) → R ∪ { +∞} afunction. Then, the following are equivalent: 

(a) ρ is spherical submodular. 

(b) ρ+ is convex. 

(c) cone ρ is an L-sublinear function and ρ = (cone ρ)|Σ◦ .In addition, for a spherical submodular function ρ, we have cone ρ = ρ+, which forms the unique L-sublinear function whose restriction to Σ◦ is ρ.Proof. The implication (c) ⇒ (a) is clear. (a) ⇒ (b). Since ρ is a spherical submodular function, there is an L-sublinear function 

σ : V → R ∪ { +∞} such that ρ is the restriction of σ to V(Σ ◦). Our aim is to show that ρ+ = σ,which implies that ρ+ is convex (and σ is the unique L-sublinear function whose restriction 38 to V(Σ ◦) is ρ). Since dom ρ+ = dom ρ+ ⊆ cone(dom ρ) ⊆ dom σ, it suffices to show that 

ρ+(x) = σ(x) for any x ∈ dom σ.Take any x ∈ dom σ. Recall that Ax denotes the unique cell of Σ ◦ such that the corresponding simplex cone Ax of Σ sp contains x. Let K ∈ Π( σ) with x ∈ K. Since K is an L-convex cone, there is a convex subcomplex Σ K of Σ sp such that K = |ΣK | by Lemma 5.3. In particular, the cell cone Ax belongs to Σ K . Since σ is linear on K, we have σ(x) = P

It follows from the definitions (2.3) and (4.12) of cone ρ and ρ+, respectively, that ρ+ ≥ cone ρ and ρ+(x) = ρ(x) for x ∈ V (Σ ◦). Since cone ρ is the pointwise maximum proper sublinear function satisfying cone ρ(x) ≤ ρ(x) for all x ∈ V (Σ ◦), we have cone ρ ≥ ρ+, which implies that ρ+ = cone ρ. Thus, it suffices to see that every member of Π( ρ+) is an L-convex cone. Take any K ∈ Π( ρ+) and x ∈ K. By the definition of ρ+, we have ρ+(x) = P 

> v∈V (Ax)

μx(v)ρ(v). Suppose that K = argmin( ρ+ − p) for p ∈ V ∗. If V(Ax)̸ ⊆ K, then ρ+(x) − ⟨ p, x ⟩ > min( ρ+ − p), which contradicts that x ∈ K. Thus, V(Ax) ⊆ K, i.e., cone Ax ⊆ K holds. Hence, K is representable as K = S

cone Ax, which implies that K is the geometric realization of a sub-complex of Σ sp . Furthermore, since K is a convex set, it is the geometric realization of a convex subcomplex by Lemma 4.9. Thus, K is an L-convex cone by Lemma 5.3. The following corollary is a direct consequence of Theorem 5.12 and Proposition 5.14. 

Corollary 5.15. A subset P ⊆ V ∗ is an (integral) M-convex polyhedron if and only if P = B(ρ)

for some (integral) spherical submodular function ρ on a spherical expression of Σsp .

Nx∗ .Recall that, for a root system Φ − with Φ − ≾ Φ, if cone ρ is an L-sublinear function of type Φ −,then it is also of type Φ. Thus, we can define the Φ -integrality for spherical submodular functions of type Φ −: For a root system Φ −, a spherical submodular function

We can characterize the Φ-integrality of (spherical) submodular functions as that of their function values in the following sense.

5.3.2 Basic properties of M-convex polyhedra 

We here summarize basic properties of M-convex polyhedra. 

Lemma 5.19. Every nonempty face of an (integral) M-convex polyhedron is an (integral) M-convex polyhedron. Proof. Let F be a nonempty face of an M-convex polyhedron P . Take any p ∈ F ; our aim is to show that T p(F ) is an M-convex cone. Then, T p(F ) is a face of T p(P ). Since T p(P ) is an M-convex cone, there is Φ ′ ⊆ Φ such that T p(P ) = cone Φ ′. Thus, the face T p(F ) is of the form cone Φ ′′ for some Φ ′′ ⊆ Φ′, which implies that T p(F ) is an M-convex cone. In addition, since any face of an L-integral polyhedron is L-integral by Lemma 2.12, we can conclude that any nonempty face of an integral M-convex polyhedron is an integral M-convex polyhedron. 41 Lemma 5.20. The union of arbitrarily many integral M-convex polyhedra is closed. Proof. By Corollary 5.15, for any integral M-convex polyhedron P , there is an integral spherical submodular function ρ : V(Σ ⋆

> sp

) → R ∪ { +∞} .Let {B(ρλ)}λ∈Λ be an arbitrary family of integral M-convex polyhedra, where ρλ is an integral spherical submodular function on Σ ⋆

> sp

. For any λ ∈ Λ and x ∈ dom ρλ, we have ρλ(x) ∈ Z/a (x)by Proposition 5.17 (1). By a similar argument to the proof of Lemma 5.11, we can show that 

M := S 

> λ∈Λ

B(ρλ) is closed. We then provide a characterization of M-convex polyhedra in addition to Corollary 5.15, which is closely related to the definition of Coxeter matroids (see Section 8.6). The maximality property (MP) for a nonempty subset S ⊆ V ∗ is as follows: (MP) For any p, q ∈ S and simple system ∆ = {α1, α 2, . . . , α n}, there exists r ∈ S with p ≤∆ r

and q ≤∆ r.

Theorem 5.21. (1) A set P ⊆ V ∗ is an M-convex polyhedron if and only if P = conv S for some nonempty set S ⊆ V ∗ having (MP) .

(2) A set P ⊆ V ∗ is an integral M-convex polyhedron if and only if P = conv B for some nonempty set B ⊆ L having (MP) .

The proof of the only-if part of (2) is deferred to Remark 7.6. 

Proof. We first show the if parts of (1) and (2). Let S ⊆ V ∗ be a nonempty set having (MP). The following claim is essential in our proof. 

Claim 5.22. The support function σ[S] of S is an L-sublinear function. In addition, if S ⊆ L ,then σ[S] is integral. Proof of Claim 5.22. Let ρ be the restriction of σ[S] to V(Σ ⋆

> sp

). It suffices to show that σ[S] = ρ+.Indeed, if σ[S] = ρ+, then ρ+ is convex, which implies that ρ is spherical submodular and 

σ[S] is L-sublinear by Proposition 5.14. In addition, if S ⊆ L , then, for x ∈ V (Σ ⋆

> sp

), we have ρ(x) = σ[S]( x) = sup {⟨ p, x ⟩ | p ∈ S} ∈ Z/a (x) ∪ { +∞} by Corollary 4.7 (1). Thus, ρ

Corollary 5.23. A nonempty polytope P ⊆ V ∗ is M-convex if and only if, for any simple system 

∆, there exists a unique maximal member p ∈ P with respect to ≤∆.

5.4 Locally polyhedral L- and M-convex functions 

In this subsection, we introduce the L- and M-convexity of locally polyhedral convex functions. For a locally polyhedral L-convex (resp. M-convex) function ˆh, one might naturally consider two definitions: The directional derivative is L-sublinear (resp. M-sublinear) at each point in dom ˆh, or each member P of Π( ˆh) is an L-convex (resp. M-convex) polyhedron. The following proposition ensures that it does not matter which definition we adopt, as they are equivalent. 

Proposition 5.24. (1) For a proper locally polyhedral convex function ˆf : V → R ∪ { +∞} ,

Π( ˆf ) consists of L-convex polyhedra if and only if ˆf ′(x; ·) forms an L-sublinear function for any x ∈ dom ˆf .

(2) For a proper locally polyhedral convex function ˆg : V ∗ → R ∪ { +∞} , Π(ˆ g) consists of M-convex polyhedra if and only if ˆg′(p; ·) forms an M-sublinear function for any p ∈ dom ˆ g.Proof. We only show the assertion (1). The proof for the assertion (2) is given by replacing “the definition” and “Lemma 5.10” with “Lemma 5.6” and “the definition,” respectively, in the following proof. 44 (Only-if part). Fix an arbitrary x ∈ dom ˆf . By Lemma 2.11 (2), we have Π( ˆf ′(x; ·)) = 

{Tx(P ) | P ∈ Π( ˆf ; x)}. Since every P ∈ Π( ˆf ; x) is an L-convex polyhedron by the assumption, its tangent cone T x(P ) at x is an L-convex cone by Lemma 5.10. Since Π( ˆf ′(x; ·)) consists of L-convex cones, ˆf ′(x; ·) is L-sublinear by the definition. (If part). Take any P ∈ Π( ˆf ) and x ∈ P . Since P is a closed convex set, it suffices to see that T x(P ) is an L-convex cone by Lemma 5.10. By Lemma 2.11 (2), we have T x(P ) ∈

Π( ˆf ′(x; ·)). Since ˆf ′(x; ·) is L-sublinear by the assumption, T x(P ) forms an L-convex cone by the definition. Hence, we say that a proper locally polyhedral convex function ˆf : V → R ∪ { +∞} is a 

locally polyhedral L-convex function of type Φ if Π( ˆf ) consists of L-convex polyhedra of type Φ, or equivalently, ˆf ′(x; ·) forms an L-sublinear function of type Φ for any x ∈ dom ˆf . In addition, it is said to be primal-integral and dual-integral if every member of Π( ˆf ) is an integral L-convex polyhedron and ˆf ′(x; ·) forms an integral L-sublinear function for any x ∈ dom ˆf ,respectively. If a locally polyhedral L-convex function is primal- and dual-integral, then it is said to be just integral . The M-convex counterparts, namely, a locally polyhedral M-convex function 

ˆg : V ∗ → R ∪ { +∞} of type Φ and its primal-integral , dual-integral , and integral variants, are defined analogously by replacing ˆf , V , and “L-convex” with ˆ g, V ∗, and “M-convex”, respectively. The following theorem (Theorem 5.25) describes the relations among the (primal-integral/dual-integral/integral) locally polyhedral L-/M-convex functions, particularly, the one-to-one corre-spondences 

• between the dual-integral locally polyhedral L-convex (resp. M-convex) functions and the primal-integral locally polyhedral M-convex (resp. L-convex) functions; and 

• between the integral locally polyhedral L- and M-convex functions via the Fenchel–Legendre conjugates. The proof is given later. 

Theorem 5.25. (1) If ˆf (resp. ˆg) is a (primal-integral) locally polyhedral L-convex (resp. M-convex) function and ˆf ∗ (resp. ˆg∗) is a locally polyhedral convex function, then ˆf ∗ (resp. 

ˆg∗) is a (dual-integral) locally polyhedral M-convex (resp. L-convex) function. 

(2) If ˆf (resp. ˆg) is a dual-integral locally polyhedral L-convex (resp. M-convex) function, then 

ˆf ∗ (resp. ˆg∗) is a primal-integral locally polyhedral M-convex (resp. L-convex) function. 

(3) If ˆf (resp. ˆg) is an integral locally polyhedral L-convex (resp. M-convex) function, then ˆf ∗

(resp. ˆg∗) is an integral locally polyhedral M-convex (resp. L-convex) function. 

Let ˆL[R → R], ˆL[Z|R → R], ˆL[R → Z|R], and ˆL[Z|R → Z|R] denote the classes of locally poly-hedral L-convex functions, and their primal-integral, dual-integral, and integral variants, respec-tively. The corresponding classes of M-convex functions are denoted by ˆM[R → R], ˆM[Z|R → R], ˆM[R → Z|R], and ˆM[Z|R → Z|R], respectively. Furthermore, the corresponding unhatted sym-bols (e.g., L[R → R], M[R → R], etc.) denote their respective subclasses consisting of functions whose conjugates are also locally polyhedral convex. Theorem 5.25 (2) and (3) imply that the conjugate of any dual-integral/integral locally polyhedral L- or M-convex function is a locally polyhedral function. Thus, we obtain ˆL[R → Z|R] = L[R → Z|R], ˆM[R → Z|R] = M[R → Z|R], ˆL[Z|R → Z|R] = L[Z|R → Z|R], and ˆM[Z|R → Z|R] = M[Z|R → Z|R]. The commutative dia-gram (6.3) below includes the relationships among these classes. The following is a sufficient condition for a given convex function to be primal-integral locally polyhedral L- or M-convex function, which is used in the proof of Theorem 5.25 (2) and several statements in Sections 6 and 7. 45 Proposition 5.26. Proposition 5.28. The effective domain of a primal-integral locally polyhedral L-convex function (resp. M-convex function) is an integral L-convex polyhedron (resp. M-convex polyhedron). Proof. We next see the assertion for a primal-integral locally polyhedral M-convex function ˆ g. It follows from dom ˆ g = |Π(ˆ g)| and Lemma 5.20 that dom ˆ g is a closed convex set. For any p ∈

dom ˆ g, we have T p(dom ˆ g) = dom(ˆ g′(p; ·)), which forms an M-convex cone by the M-sublinearity of ˆ g′(p; ·). Thus, by the definition, the effective domain dom ˆ g is an M-convex polyhedron. Hence, it suffices to show dom ˆ g = conv(dom ˆ g ∩ L ) for the integrality of dom ˆ g. The inclusion dom ˆ g ⊇ conv(dom ˆ g ∩ L ) clearly holds. # 6 Discrete convexity over combinatorial objects 

Building upon the geometric formulations in Section 5, this section formally defines discrete convexity over combinatorial objects. Sections 6.1 and 6.2 introduce L- and M-convex sets and functions over the vertex sets of Euclidean Coxeter complexes and root lattices, respectively, and their integral ones. Section 6.3 establishes the discrete Fenchel–Legendre conjugacy between integral L-convex functions and integral M-convex functions. 47 6.1 Definitions of L-convex sets and functions 

In this subsection, we assume that Φ is an irreducible root system. Recall that P ⊆ V is an integral L-convex polyhedron of type Φ if and only if P = |Σ′| for some nonempty convex subcomplex Σ ′ of Σ Euc (Lemma 5.5). A nonempty subset D ⊆ V is said to be L-convex of type Φ if it is the vertex set of some nonempty convex subcomplex Σ ′ of Σ Euc , or equivalently, if there exists an integral L-convex polyhedron P such that D = P ∩V . This P is uniquely determined as P = D = conv D = conv D,since P = |Σ′| = D ⊆ conv D ⊆ conv D ⊆ P , where the last inclusion follows from the fact that conv D is the inclusion-wise minimum closed convex set that contains D. For later reference, we summarize this fact. 

Corollary 6.1. For an L-convex set D ⊆ V , we have D = conv D = conv D, which forms an integral L-convex polyhedron. 

By combining the above arguments with those in Section 5.2.1, we obtain the following commutative diagram, describing relations among L0[R], L0[Z|R], 0M[R → R], 0M[R → Z|R], ˇT [R], T [R], and T [Z] with the family L0[Z] of L-convex sets. ˇT [R]

T [R]

L0[R] 0M[R → R]

L0[Z] T [Z]

L0[Z|R] 0M[R → Z|R]

> D(·)cone( ·)
> σ[·]
> D(·)
> D(·)(σ[·]) |Φ
> cone( ·)(·)|Φ
> D(·)(σ[·]) |Φ
> cone( ·)(·)|Φ
> σ[·]
> D(·)(·)=conv( ·)=conv( ·)(·)∩V
> (σ[·]) |Φ
> D(·)∩V

(6.1) A function f : V → R ∪ { +∞} is said to be L-convex of type Φ if there is a primal-integral locally polyhedral L-convex function ˆf : V → R ∪ { +∞} such that f is the restriction of ˆf to 

V. An L-convex function f : V → R ∪ { +∞} is said to be integral if it is the restriction of an integral locally polyhedral L-convex function. 

Proposition 6.2. For an L-convex function f : V → R ∪ { +∞} , we have f = conv f = conv f ,which forms the unique primal-integral locally polyhedral L-convex function whose restriction to 

V is f .Proof. Let f : V → R ∪ { +∞} be an L-convex function and suppose that ˆf : V → R ∪ { +∞} 

is a primal-integral locally polyhedral L-convex function satisfying f = ˆf |V . Since ˆf is a closed convex function that minorizes f , we have ˆf ≤ conv f ≤ conv f ≤ f , where the last inequality follows from the definitions of conv f and f . Thus, it suffices to show f = ˆf .We first see that dom ˆf = dom f . Since dom ˆf is an integral L-convex polyhedron by Proposition 5.28, there is a nonempty convex subcomplex Σ f of Σ Euc with dom ˆf = |Σf |

6.2 Definitions of M-convex sets and functions 

Recall that an integral M-convex polyhedron P is an M-convex polyhedron having the L-integrality, i.e., P = conv( P ∩ L ); see Section 5.3. A nonempty subset B ⊆ L is said to be M-convex if B is the set of lattice points in some integral M-convex polyhedron P , i.e., B = P ∩ L for some integral M-convex polyhedron P .This is equivalent to the condition that conv B is an integral M-convex polyhedron and B =(conv B) ∩ L .For an M-convex set B ⊆ L , we have conv B = conv B, since conv B forms a polyhedron. Thus, with the consideration of Section 5.3.1, we obtain the following commutative diagram describing the relations among M0[R], M0[Z], 0L[R → R], 0L[R → Z|R], SΣ◦ [R], SΣ◦ [Z] with the family M0[Z] of M-convex sets.

(6.2) 50 A function g : L → R ∪ { +∞} is said to be M-convex if there is a primal-integral locally polyhedral M-convex function ˆ g : V ∗ → R ∪ { +∞} such that g is the restriction of ˆ g to L. An M-convex function g : L → R ∪ { +∞} is said to be integral if it is the restriction of an integral locally polyhedral M-convex function.

Theorem 6.7. For an M-convex function g : L → R ∪ { +∞} , it is integral if and only if γg,p is integral for all p ∈ dom g.

For B ⊆ L and p ∈ B, we define Φp(B) := {α ∈ Φ | p + α ∈ B},

which plays a role as the tangent cone of B at p in the following sense. 

Proposition 6.8. For an M-convex set B ⊆ L and p ∈ B, we have Tp(conv B) = cone(Φ p(B)) .

The above proposition (Proposition 6.8) is the main bridge between the continuous and discrete sides of M-convexity, which shows that the tangent cone of the convex hull of an M-convex set is completely generated by feasible increments along single roots. This identity is used repeatedly in the local optimality theorem and in the intrinsic characterizations of M-convex sets and functions in Section 7. The proof of Proposition 6.8 requires further properties of spherical submodular functions, which are of independent interest, with type-dependent arguments, and hence we defer it to Section 6.4. We further note that this is the main point at which the classical-type assumption enters the discrete M-convex theory. An analogue of this identity for the exceptional root systems would extend the subsequent M-convex results to those types. 6.3 Conjugacy between integral L-convex and M-convex functions 

For a function f : V → R ∪ { +∞} with dom f̸ = ∅, its discrete Fenchel–Legendre conjugate 

f • : L → R ∪ { +∞} is the function defined by 

f •(p) := sup {⟨ p, x ⟩ − f (x) | x ∈ dom f } (p ∈ L ).

Similarly, for a function g : L → R ∪ { +∞} with dom g̸ = ∅, its discrete Fenchel–Legendre conjugate g• : V → R ∪ { +∞} is the function defined by 

g•(x) := sup {⟨ p, x ⟩ − g(p) | p ∈ dom g} (x ∈ V ).

We note that f • (resp. g•) is the restriction of f ∗ to L (resp. g∗ to V). The following establishes the one-to-one correspondence between the integral L-convex func-tions and the integral M-convex functions via the discrete Fenchel–Legendre conjugate. 

Theorem 6.11. Let f : V → R ∪ { +∞} and g : L → R ∪ { +∞} be functions. 

(1) If f is an integral L-convex function, then its discrete Fenchel–Legendre conjugate f • is an integral M-convex function and f = ( f •)•.

52 (2) If g is an integral M-convex function, then its discrete Fenchel–Legendre conjugate g• is an integral L-convex function and g = ( g•)•.Proof. We only show the assertion (1); the same argument yields the assertion (2). For an integral L-convex function f : V → R ∪ { +∞} , we have 

f • = ( f ∗)|L = ( f ∗∗∗ )|L = (conv f )∗|L,

where the second and last equalities follow from Lemma 2.3. Since conv f is an integral locally polyhedral L-convex function by Proposition 6.2, its conjugate (conv f )∗ forms an integral locally polyhedral M-convex function by Theorem 5.25 (3). Thus, f • = (conv f )∗|L is an integral M-convex function. Furthermore, since conv( f •) = (conv f )∗ by Proposition 6.6 and the above argument, we obtain (f •)• = (conv( f •)) ∗|V = (conv f )∗∗ |V = (conv f )|V = f, 

where the penultimate and last equalities follow from Lemma 2.3 (2) and Proposition 6.2, re-spectively. Let L[Z → R] and L[Z → Z] be the classes of L-convex functions and of integral L-convex functions, respectively; the corresponding classes of M-convex functions are denoted by M[Z → R]and M[Z → Z]. By combining the above arguments with those in Section 5.4, we obtain the following commutative diagram. ˆL[R → R] ˆM[R → R]

Lemma 6.14. Let ρ : V(Σ ◦) → R ∪ { +∞} be a spherical submodular function and p ∈ B(ρ).

(1) Σ sp [ρ = p] is a convex subcomplex of Σsp . In particular, Σsp [ρ = p] and Σ◦[ρ = p] are chamber complexes. In addition, let A be a maximal simplex in Σ◦[ρ = p].

(2) aff B(ρ)p = p + span Φ |−A. In particular, dim B(ρ)p = n − #V(A).

We show the former statement. By Lemma 4.9, it suffices to see that |Σsp [ρ = p]| is convex. Since p ∈ B(ρ), we have ⟨p, x ⟩ ≤ ρ(x) for all x ∈ V (Σ ◦). Thus, the set {x ∈ V (Σ ◦) | ρ(x) = ⟨p, x ⟩} 

coincides with argmin( ρ − p). Since ρ is spherical submodular, ρ+ is convex by Proposi-tion 5.14, implying that argmin( ρ+ − p) forms a convex set. = p + (span |Σsp [ρ = p]|)⊥. (6.8) Moreover, since p ∈ B(ρ), Σ sp [ρ = p] and Σ ◦[ρ = p] are chamber complexes by the assertion (1). Since A is a maximal simplex of Σ ◦[ρ = p], the simplex cone A of Σ sp [ρ = p] corresponding to 

A is also maximal in Σ sp [ρ = p]. Thus, we have dim cone A = dim |Σsp [ρ = p]|, implying that span(cone A) = span |Σsp [ρ = p]|. Since (span(cone A)) ⊥ = span Φ |−A by Lemma 4.3 (1), we obtain aff B(ρ)p = p + (span |Σsp [ρ = p]|)⊥ = p + span Φ |−A.(3). Since B(ρ)p = B(ρ) ∩ aff B(ρ)p, we obtain 

B(ρ)p = B(ρ) ∩ (p + span Φ |−A)= {q ∈ V ∗ | q − p ∈ span Φ |−A and ⟨q, x ⟩ ≤ ρ(x) ( ∀x ∈ V (Σ ◦)) }

= p + {q ∈ span Φ |−A | ⟨ q, x ⟩ ≤ (ρ − p)( x) ( ∀x ∈ V (Σ ◦)) }⊆ p + {q ∈ span Φ |−A | ⟨ q, x ⟩ ≤ (ρ − p)( x) ( ∀x ∈ V (Lk Σ◦ (A))) }. (6.9) Here, the first equality follows from aff B(ρ)p = p + span Φ |−A by the assertion (2). On the other hand, since A is a simplex in Σ ◦[ρ = p], we have ( ρ−p)( x) = 0 for any x ∈ V (A). Thus, ( ρ − p)/A is a spherical submodular function on Σ ◦/A by Lemma 6.13, which allows us to consider the base polyhedron B(( ρ − p)/A ). Since Σ ◦/A is a spherical expression of Σ sp (Φ |−A), 

B(( ρ − p)/A ) lies in span Φ |−A. More precisely, B(( ρ − p)/A ) is representable as 

B(( ρ − p)/A ) = {q ∈ span Φ |−A | ⟨ q, x ⟩ ≤ (ρ − p)( x) ( ∀x ∈ V (Lk Σ◦ (A))) }, (6.10) since (( ρ − p)/A )( x + span A) = ( ρ − p)( x) for x ∈ V (Lk Σ◦ (A)) by the definition of ( ρ − p)/A 

(see (6.4)) and ⟨q, x + span A⟩ = ⟨q, x ⟩ for q ∈ span Φ |−A = (span(cone A)) ⊥ = (span A)⊥ by Lemma 4.3 (1). By (6.9) and (6.10), it suffices to show that the inclusion (6.9) can be replaced with the identity. To this end, we show the following claim. 

Claim 6.15. (1) If q belongs to a proper face of B(ρ)p, i.e., B(ρ)q ⊆ B(ρ)p and dim B(ρ)q <

dim B(ρ)p, then there exists x ∈ V (Lk Σ◦ (A)) such that ρ(x) = q(x).

(2) ( ρ − p)( x) > 0 for all x ∈ V (Lk Σ◦ (A)) .Proof of Claim 6.15. (1). Since q ∈ B(ρ)p, we have V(Σ ◦[ρ = p]) ⊆ V (Σ ◦[ρ = q]) (see (6.7)), that is, Σ ◦[ρ = p] is a subcomplex of Σ ◦[ρ = q]. In addition, it follows from dim B(ρ)q < dim B(ρ)p

and the equation (6.8) that dim |Σsp [ρ = p]| < dim |Σsp [ρ = q]|. This implies that the rank of Σ◦[ρ = q] is strictly larger than that of Σ ◦[ρ = p]; recall that Σ ◦[ρ = p] (resp. Σ◦[ρ = q]) is isomorphic to Σ sp [ρ = p] (resp. Σ sp [ρ = q]), and that Σ ◦[ρ = p] and Σ ◦[ρ = q] are chamber complexes by the assertion (1). Hence, A is a simplex of Σ ◦[ρ = q], but not maximal in Σ ◦[ρ = q], i.e., there is a simplex A′ ∈ Σ◦[ρ = q] with V(A′) ⊋ V(A). Since A is a face of A′, any vertex 

x ∈ V (A′) \ V (A)̸ = ∅ belongs to V(Lk Σ◦ (A)); this x satisfies ρ(x) = q(x). (2). If there is x ∈ V (Lk Σ◦ (A)) with ρ(x) = p(x), then the join of A and x is a simplex of Σ◦[ρ = p], which contradicts that A is a maximal simplex in Σ ◦[ρ = p]. Suppose to the contrary that the inclusion (6.9) is strict, i.e., there is q′ ∈ span Φ |−A such that ⟨q′, x ⟩ ≤ (ρ − p)( x) for all x ∈ V (Lk Σ◦ (A)) but p + q′ /∈ B(ρ)p. Let μ be the maximum real satisfying p + μq ′ ∈ B(ρ)p; since p belongs to a relative interior of B(ρ)p and p + q′ ∈ (aff B(ρ)p) \

B(ρ)p, we have 0 < μ < 1. For this μ, the point p + μq ′ ∈ p + span Φ |−A belongs to a proper face of B(ρ)p. Hence, by Claim 6.15 (1), there is x ∈ Lk Σ◦ (A) such that ρ(x) = ( p + μq ′)( x), or equivalently, μq ′(x) = ( ρ − p)( x). Since ( ρ − p)( x) > 0 by Claim 6.15 (2) and 0 < μ < 1, we obtain q′(x) > (ρ − p)( x). This contradicts the assumption of q′.56 Suppose that Φ = Φ 1 ⊕ Φ2 ⊕ · · · ⊕ Φk, in which Φ 1, Φ2, . . . , Φk are irreducible components of Φ. Corollary 6.21. Let Φ be an irreducible root system, B an M-convex set of type Φ, and p ∈ B.Suppose that α ∈ Φ belongs to Tp(conv B), and let ε := sup {ε′ | p + ε′α ∈ conv B}. Then, the following hold. 

63 (1) If Φ is of type A, then ε ≥ 1, i.e., p + α ∈ B.

(2) If Φ is of type B, C, or D, then either ε ≥ 1, i.e., p + α ∈ B, or ε = 1 /2 and there are two roots α+, α − ∈ Φ such that p + α+, p + α− ∈ B and α = α+ + α−.

# 7 Characterizations of discrete convexity 

In this section, we provide combinatorial characterizations for the discrete convex sets and func-tions defined in Section 6. Sections 7.1 and 7.2 characterize L-convex sets and functions in terms of the discrete midpoint convexity. Section 7.3 characterizes M-convex sets and functions through hole-freeness and the maximality property. Finally, Section 7.4 characterizes M-convex functions based on the M-convexity of their effective domains, the M-sublinearity of their (discrete version of) directional derivatives, and a local-to-global lower bound condition. 

7.1 L-convex sets 

The following theorem summarizes characterizations of L-convex sets, including the discrete midpoint convexity corresponding to the condition (d) below. 

Theorem 7.1. For a nonempty set D ⊆ V , the following are equivalent: 

(a) D is an L-convex set. 

(b) Σ Euc [D] is a convex subcomplex of ΣEuc 

(c) D is convex in V .

(d) V(A(x+y)/2) ⊆ D for x, y ∈ D.Proof. (a) ⇔ (b) ⇔ (c) immediately follows from the definition of L-convexity and Lemmas 4.9 and 5.5. (c) ⇒ (d). Recall that D is the geometric realization |ΣEuc [D]| of the subcomplex Σ Euc [D]induced by D. Take any x, y ∈ D. Since D is convex in V , we have ( x + y)/2 ∈ D = |ΣEuc [D]|,which implies A(x+y)/2 ∈ ΣEuc [D], i.e., V(A(x+y)/2) ⊆ D.(d) ⇒ (a). Let D ⊆ V be a nonempty set satisfying the condition in the assertion (d). It suffices to show that Σ Euc [D] is convex, i.e., for any A, B ∈ ΣEuc [D], we have AB ∈ ΣEuc [D]. Indeed, if Σ Euc [D] is convex, then |ΣEuc [D]| is an integral L-convex polyhedron by Lemma 5.5, and hence D = |ΣEuc [D]| ∩ V is an L-convex set. We prove this by induction on d(A, B ). If d(A, B ) = 0, then A and B are joinable and 

V(AB ) = V(A) ∪ V (B). Hence we obtain AB ∈ Σ[ D], since V(A) ∪ V (B) ⊆ D.Suppose that d(A, B ) > 0. We consider the following three cases: (i) A and B are vertices, (ii) A is a vertex, and (iii) A and B are general simplices. (i). Suppose that V(A) = {x} and V(B) = {y}. We first observe that, for any w ∈ (x, y ), we have xy = xA w. Indeed, the convex combination (1 − ε)x + εy for sufficiently small ε > 0 can be simulated by some convex combination of x and w. Set z := ( x+y)/2 ∈ (x, y ). The condition (d) says that Az ∈ Σ[ D]. By Lemma 4.12 (7), we have d(x, y ) ≥ d(x, A z ). If d(x, y ) > d (x, A z ), then by induction hypothesis we have Σ[ D] ∋ xA z = xy . If d(x, y ) = d(x, A z ), then Lemma 4.12 (7) says that x is a vertex of Az , which implies that xy = xA z = Az . Hence xy ∈ Σ[ D] holds. (ii). Suppose that A = x and V(B) = {y0, y 1, . . . , y k}. Since d(x, B ) ≥ d(x, y i), by induction hypothesis and the argument in (i) we have xy i ∈ Σ[ D] for each i. Furthermore, xy 0, xy 1, . . . , xy k

are joinable and its join is ( xy 0)( xy 1) · · · (xy k) = xy 0 · · · yk = AB , in which the first equality 64 follows from Lemma 4.12 (2) and the second follows from A = x and B = y0y1 . . . y k. Hence we obtain AB ∈ Σ[ D]. (iii). Suppose that V(A) = {x0, . . . , x k} and V(B) = {y0, . . . , y ℓ}. Then, we have d(A, B ) = 

d(x0x1 · · · xk, B ) ≥ d(x0x1 . . . x k−1, x kB) ≥ · · · ≥ d(x0, x 1 · · · xkB), where the inequalities follow from Lemma 4.12 (4). Hence, by induction hypothesis and the argument in (ii), we obtain 

x0x1 · · · xkB = AB ∈ Σ[ D]. Using the explicit descriptions of V(A(x+y)/2) for x, y ∈ V (Σ Euc (Φ)) given in Appendix B, we can rephrase the condition (d) of Theorem 7.1 with more explicit formulae for Φ An , Φ Bn , Φ Cn ,and Φ Dn , which are given in Corollary B.5. 

7.2 L-convex functions 

As in the case of L-convex sets, we can characterize L-convex functions by the discrete midpoint convexity. Here, the discrete midpoint convexity is referred to as the condition for a function 

f : V → R ∪ { +∞} with dom f̸ = ∅ that, for each x, y ∈ V , it satisfies 

f (x) + f (y) ≥ 2 X

> v∈V

λz (v)f (v), (7.1) where z is the midpoint ( x + y)/2 of x and y.

Theorem 7.2. For a function f : V → R ∪ { +∞} with dom f̸ = ∅, the following are equivalent: 

(a) f is L-convex. 

(b) f is convex in V .

(c) f satisfies the discrete midpoint convexity (7.1) .

(d) dom f is L-convex and f satisfies the discrete midpoint convexity (7.1) for any two adjacent maximal simplices A1, A 2 of ΣEuc [dom f ], x ∈ V (A1) \ V (A2), and y ∈ V (A2) \ V (A1).Proof. The implication (a) ⇒ (b) immediately follows from Proposition 6.2. (b) ⇒ (a). Since the affine hyperplane arrangement Haff (Φ) is locally finite, i.e., every point of V has a neighborhood that meets only finitely many affine hyperplanes in Haff (Φ) (see e.g., [1, Definition 10.7]) and f is affine on the closure of each simplex, we can conclude that f is locally polyhedral by its convexity. Let P = argmin( f − p) ∈ Π( f ) and take x ∈ P . Since f − p is affine on cl Ax and attains its minimum at x ∈ Ax = relint(cl Ax), every vertex of Ax also belongs to P . Thus, P is the geometric realization of a subcomplex of Σ Euc . In addition, since P is convex, this subcomplex is convex by Lemma 4.9, and hence P is an integral L-convex polyhedron by Lemma 5.5. Therefore, 

f is a primal-integral locally polyhedral L-convex function. Since f = f |V , we conclude that f

is L-convex. (b) ⇒ (c). By the definition of f and its convexity, for x, y ∈ V , we have f (x) + f (y) = 

f (x) + f (y) ≥ 2f (( x + y)/2) = 2 P 

> v∈V

λ(x+y)/2(v)f (v). (c) ⇒ (d). The discrete midpoint convexity (7.1) of f implies that V(A(x+y)/2) ⊆ dom f for any x, y ∈ dom f . Thus, by Theorem 7.1, dom f is an L-convex set. The latter statement is immediate. (d) ⇒ (b). Suppose that f satisfies the condition (d). Recall that dom f = |ΣEuc [dom f ]|

by the definition of the Lov´ asz extension. Since dom f is an L-convex set, Σ Euc [dom f ] is a nonempty convex subcomplex of Σ Euc by Theorem 7.1, implying that Σ Euc [dom f ] is a chamber complex by Lemma 4.11. We see the following claim: 65 Claim 7.3. For any two adjacent maximal simplices (chambers) A1, A 2 of ΣEuc [dom f ], the set 

cl A1 ∪ cl A2 is convex. In addition, the restriction of f to cl A1 ∪ cl A2 is convex. Proof of Claim 7.3. After restricting the affine hyperplane arrangement Haff (Φ) to aff |ΣEuc [dom f ]|,the simplices A1 and A2 are adjacent chambers separated by H := aff(cl A1 ∩ cl A2), implying that their sign sequences differ only at this hyperplane, or equivalently, that they are strictly separated by exactly one hyperplane (see, e.g., [1, Proposition 3.78]). Hence, cl A1 ∪ cl A2 is the intersection of the closed half-spaces determined by their common signs at all hyperplanes other than H, and is therefore convex. Since f is affine on cl A1 and on cl A2, the restriction of f to cl A1 ∪ cl A2 is the piecewise affine function consisting of two pieces cl A1 and cl A2. Let ℓ1 (resp. ℓ2) denote the affine extension of f |cl A1 (resp. f |cl A2 ) to aff |ΣEuc [dom f ]|. Then, we have ℓ1(z) = ℓ2(z) for z ∈ H.Let x ∈ V (A1) \ V (A2) and y ∈ V (A2) \ V (A1); we may assume that ( x + y)/2 ∈ cl A1. The condition (d), or more precisely, the discrete midpoint convexity (7.1) for x and y, implies that 

ℓ1(x) + ℓ2(y) = f (x) + f (y) ≥ 2f (( x + y)/2) = 2 ℓ1(( x + y)/2) = ℓ1(x) + ℓ1(y).

Thus, ℓ2(y) ≥ ℓ1(y), or equivalently, ℓ2(z) ≥ ℓ1(z) holds for z ∈ cl A2. In addition, since the affine function ℓ2 − ℓ1 vanishes on the hyperplane H and A1 and A2 lie on opposite sides of H,the inequality ℓ1(z) ≥ ℓ2(z) holds for z ∈ cl A1. Thus, we obtain f (z) = max {ℓ1(z), ℓ 2(z)} for 

z ∈ cl A1 ∪ cl A2, which proves that f is convex on cl A1 ∪ cl A2.Take any a, b ∈ dom f = |ΣEuc [dom f ]|; our aim is to show that f is convex on the closed segment [ a, b ], which implies that f is convex. Note that [ a, b ] ⊆ dom f by the convexity of dom f . In the following argument, codimension is taken relative to |ΣEuc [dom f ]|.We first consider the case where the segment [ a, b ] is contained in the closure of some simplex, or the relative interior ( a, b ) meets no simplex of codimension at least two; we say that the pair (a, b ) is in simple position in this case. By the local finiteness of the affine hyperplane arrangement Haff (Φ), the segment [ a, b ] meets only finitely many affine hyperplanes. Thus, there are distinct points a =: a0, a 1, . . . , a k := b

appearing in this order on [ a, b ] and maximal simplices A1, A 2, . . . , A k of Σ Euc [dom f ] such that [ai−1, a i] ⊆ cl Ai. If k = 1, i.e., [ a, b ] is included in the closure of some simplex, then f is affine, and hence, convex on [ a, b ]. Suppose that k ≥ 2. By the assumption, Ai and Ai+1 are adjacent for every i ∈ [k − 1]. It follows from Claim 7.3 and the inclusion [ ai−1, a i+1 ] ⊆ cl Ai ∪ cl Ai+1 

that f is convex on [ a0, a 2], [a1, a 3], [a2, a 4], . . . , [ak−2, a k]. Since consecutive intervals overlap in a nondegenerate interval, these convexity properties can be successively glued together. Thus, f

is convex on [ a0, a k] = [ a, b ]. We then consider the case where the pair ( a, b ) is not in simple position, i.e., [ a, b ] is not contained in the closure of a single simplex and its relative interior meets some simplex of codimension at least two. By the local finiteness of Haff (Φ), there are only finitely many simplices of Σ Euc [dom f ] that meet conv( {a} ∪ B(b; ε)), where B(b; ε) denotes the open ball centered at 

b with radius ε. Let B1, B 2, . . . , B k be all of such simplices of Σ Euc [dom f ] having codimension at least two. Then, the set a + cone( Bi − a) is of codimension at least one for each i. Let 

B′ := ( B(b; ε) ∩ | ΣEuc [dom f ]|) \ Ski=1 (a + cone( Bi − a)). Since B′ is a nonempty relatively open subset of aff |ΣEuc [dom f ]|, it cannot be covered by a finite union of subsets of codimension at least one. Therefore, we obtain B′̸ = ∅. By the definition of B′, the pair ( a, b ′) is in simple position for any b′ ∈ B′.By the above argument, there is a sequence ( bm)m=1 ,2,... such that ( a, b m) is in simple position for all m and lim m→∞ bm = b. Since [ a, b m] is in simple position, for every λ ∈ [0 , 1], we have (1 −λ)f (a)+ λf (bm) ≥ f ((1 −λ)a+λb m). Since f is continuous on |ΣEuc [dom f ]|, letting m → ∞ ,we obtain (1 − λ)f (a) + λf (b) ≥ f ((1 − λ)a + λb ), which implies that f is convex on [ a, b ]. 66 Remark 7.4. We can show the implication (c) ⇒ (b) of Theorem 5.16 as follows. Fix x∗ ∈V and let ρ : N x∗ → R ∪ { +∞} be a function with ρ(x∗) < +∞ satisfying the submodular inequality (5.3). Let ρV be the function obtained from ρ by extending the domain to V, i.e., 

ρV (x) := 

(

ρ(x) if x ∈ Nx∗ ,

+∞ otherwise .

Then, ρV clearly satisfies the discrete midpoint inequality (7.1), which implies that ρV is convex in 

V by (c) ⇒ (b) of Theorem 7.2. Thus, ρ is convex, since ρ is the restriction of ρV to |St ΣEuc (x∗)|,where we recall that |St ΣEuc (x∗)| is a convex set by Lemma 4.10. ■

As in Section 7.1, using the explicit descriptions of λz ∈ ΛV(Az ) for x, y ∈ V (Σ Euc (Φ)) with 

z := ( x + y)/2 given in Appendix B, we can rephrase the condition (d) of Theorem 7.2 with more explicit formulae for Φ An , Φ Bn , Φ Cn , and Φ Dn , which are given in Corollary B.6. 

7.3 M-convex sets 

We summarize characterizations of M-convex sets, including the maximality property (MP) (corresponding to the condition (c)) and the discrete tangent-cone property (corresponding to the condition (d)), as follows. Here, a subset B ⊆ L is said to be hole-free if B = (conv B) ∩ L .

Theorem 7.5. For a nonempty set B ⊆ L , the following are equivalent: 

(a) B is an M-convex set. 

(b) B = B(ρ) ∩ L for some integral spherical submodular function ρ.

(c) B is a hole-free set having the maximality property (MP) .

(d) B is a hole-free set and q−p ∈ cone(Φ p(B)) for any p, q ∈ B, i.e., there exist β1, β 2, . . . , β k ∈

Φp(B) and nonnegative reals μ1, μ 2, . . . , μ k ∈ R+ such that p + Pkℓ=1 μℓβℓ = q.Proof. (a) ⇔ (b) follows from Corollary 5.15. (a) ⇒ (d). Let B be an M-convex set. The hole-freeness of B is clear. Since conv B is an integral M-convex polyhedron, for any p, q ∈ B, we have q − p ∈ Tp(conv B) = cone(Φ p(B)), where the identity follows from Proposition 6.8. Thus, the assertion (d) holds. (d) ⇒ (c). It suffices to show that B has the maximality property (MP). Fix any sim-ple system ∆ = {α1, α 2, . . . , α n} and take any p = Pni=1 piαi, q = Pni=1 qiαi ∈ B. Let 

r = Pni=1 riαi := p. By the assumption, there exist β1, β 2, . . . , β k ∈ Φ and nonnegative re-als μ1, μ 2, . . . , μ k ∈ R+ satisfying 

r +

> k

X

> ℓ=1

μℓβℓ = q and r + βℓ ∈ B (ℓ ∈ [k]) .

Suppose that ri < q i. Since r + Pkℓ=1 μℓβℓ = q, there is at least one of β1, β 2, . . . , β k, say 

β1 = Pni=1 biαi, such that bi > 0. Hence we obtain that ri < r i + bi and β1 is a positive root with respect to ∆, i.e., r < ∆ r + β1. We update r ← r + β1 ∈ B. Since each positive root has nonnegative integral coefficients with respect to ∆, the value Pni=1 max {qi − ri, 0} decreases by at least one at each update. Hence, the procedure terminates after finitely many steps; we finally obtain r ∈ B such that r ≥∆ p and r ≥∆ q. Thus, B has (MP). (c) ⇒ (a). By the if part of Theorem 5.21 (2), which has been already shown, we have conv B is an integral M-convex polyhedron. Since B is hole-free by the assumption, we obtain 

B = (conv B) ∩ L , which implies that B is an M-convex set, i.e., the assertion (a). 67 Remark 7.6. We are ready to complete the proof of Theorem 5.21 (2). If P is an integral M-convex polyhedron, then B := P ∩ L forms an M-convex set and it has (MP) by (a) ⇒ (c) of Theorem 7.5. Since P = conv B, we obtain the only-if part of Theorem 5.21 (2). ■

7.4 M-convex functions 

The following theorem provides an intrinsic characterization of M-convex functions, in terms of the discrete tangent-cone property related to the condition (d) of Theorem 7.5. 

Theorem 7.7. A function g : L → R ∪ { +∞} with dom g̸ = ∅ is M-convex if and only if it satisfies the following conditions: 

• dom g is an M-convex set; 

• for any p ∈ dom g, the function γg,p is valid, or equivalently, cone γg,p is M-sublinear; and 

• for any p, q ∈ dom g, we have g(q) ≥ g(p) + (cone γg,p )( q − p), or equivalently, there exist

Remark 7.10. The condition that dom g is an M-convex set in Theorem 7.7 can be replaced by the condition that dom g is a nonempty hole-free subset of L. Indeed, the nonempty hole-freeness of dom g and the third condition (7.2) in Theorem 7.7 imply that dom g forms an M-convex set by the implication (d) ⇒ (a) in Theorem 7.5. ■

# 8 Connections with existing concepts 

In this section, we clarify how the discrete convexity notions introduced in this paper are re-lated to existing classes. Our purpose is to determine when the present notions coincide with known ones and, when they do not, to identify the precise differences in their discrete domains, integrality conditions, and conditions on function values. In type A, the present framework recovers the original DCA, including L ♮- and M ♮-convex functions and ordinary submodular functions. In types B, C, and D, we compare the present notions with (valuated) ∆-matroids, (valuated) even ∆-matroids, bisubmodular functions, BS-convex sets and functions, and UJ-convex functions. For general root systems, we compare M-convex polytopes and spherical submodular functions with Coxeter matroids and Coxeter submodular functions, respectively. These comparisons show which existing notions are recovered as special cases of the present framework and where the present definitions differ from them. Several of these correspondences require a change of domain or a normalization, such as taking the quotient by the all-one direction, passing to a zero-sum lattice, or rescaling the representative points on the vertices of a spherical Coxeter complex. We make these identifications explicit and distinguish the real-valued and integral settings whenever the corresponding notions of integrality differ. Throughout this section, the symbol ≃ denotes the natural correspondence induced by the identification or normalization under consideration. The integrality comparisons and explicit midpoint descriptions used below rely on Section A.2 and Appendix B. 

8.1 Original DCA 

We compare the L- and M-convex functions of type Φ An introduced in this paper with the corresponding function classes in the original DCA [47, 48]. 

8.1.1 L- and L ♮-convex functions 

We first present formal definitions of L- and L ♮-convex functions. A function ˜f : Zn+1 →

R ∪ { +∞} with dom ˜f̸ = ∅ is said to be L-convex if it satisfies the following two conditions: 72 • It is linear in the direction 1n+1 , i.e., there is r ∈ R such that ˜f (x + 1n+1 ) = ˜f (x) + r (x ∈ Zn+1 ). (8.1) 

• It satisfies the discrete midpoint convexity, i.e., ˜f (x) + ˜f (y) ≥ ˜f

 x + y

2

 

+ ˜f

 x + y

2

 

(x, y ∈ Zn+1 ). (8.2) Here, for a real number r ∈ R, let ⌊r⌋ and ⌈r⌉ denote the floor function (rounding down to the nearest integer) and the ceiling function (rounding up to the nearest integer), respectively. Afunction f on Zn+1 is said to be translation-invariant if f (x + 1n+1 ) = f (x) for all x ∈ Zn+1 .A translation-invariant L-convex function is exactly an L-convex function with r = 0 in the condition (8.1). A function f : Zn → R ∪ { +∞} with dom f̸ = ∅ is said to be L♮-convex if there is a translation-invariant L-convex function ˜f : Zn+1 → R ∪ { +∞} such that f (x) = ˜f (x, 0) for all x ∈ Zn. In other words, an L ♮-convex function on Zn is the restriction of a translation-invariant L-convex function on Zn+1 . It is well known [48, Theorem 7.7] that the discrete midpoint convexity (8.2) characterizes the L ♮-convexity, i.e., a function f : Zn → R ∪ { +∞} 

with dom f̸ = ∅ is L ♮-convex if and only if it satisfies (8.2). An L-convex or L ♮-convex function is said to be integral if it is integer-valued. The classes of (integral) translation-invariant L-convex functions and (integral) L ♮-convex functions are essentially equivalent. A translation-invariant function on Zn+1 can be regarded as a function on the primal discrete domain V(Σ Euc (Φ An )) for type Φ An , where V(Σ Euc (Φ An )) = Zn+1 / span 1n+1 (see Table 5). Indeed, for a translation-invariant function ˜f on Zn+1 , we define the function on Zn+1 / span 1n+1 

by [ x] 7 → ˜f (x) for x ∈ Zn+1 and [ x] := x + span 1n+1 ∈ Zn+1 / span 1n+1 , which is well defined. Conversely, every function on this quotient has a unique translation-invariant lift to Zn+1 through the quotient map. The above argument with the characterization of the L-convexity in terms of the discrete midpoint convexity (given in Corollary B.6) immediately implies that the class of translation-invariant L-convex functions naturally corresponds to that of L-convex functions of type Φ An .Specifically, for a translation-invariant L-convex function ˜f : Zn+1 → R ∪ { +∞} , the func-tion [ x] 7 → ˜f (x) for x ∈ Zn+1 is L-convex of type Φ An . Conversely, for an L-convex function 

f : V(Σ Euc (Φ An )) → R ∪ { +∞} of type Φ An , the function x 7 → f ([ x]) for x ∈ Zn+1 forms a translation-invariant L-convex function on Zn+1 . Therefore, we obtain the following: 

{translation-invariant L-convex functions on Zn+1 }≃ { L♮-convex functions on Zn}≃ { L-convex functions of type Φ An }.

We next make the correspondence for integral functions precise. Let ˜f : Zn+1 → R ∪ { +∞} be a translation-invariant L-convex function, and f : V(Σ Euc (Φ An )) → R ∪ { +∞} the corresponding L-convex function of type Φ An , i.e., f ([ x]) = ˜f (x) for x ∈ Zn+1 . By Theorem 6.4 (2), the function 

f is integral if and only if there is c ∈ R such that f ([ x]) − c ∈ Z for all [ x] ∈ dom f . Under the above correspondence, this condition is equivalent to ˜f (x) − c ∈ Z for all x ∈ dom ˜f . Thus, if ˜f

is integral in the standard sense, i.e., if it is integer-valued, then f is integral in the sense of this paper. Conversely, if f is integral, then the translation-invariant lift of f − c is an integer-valued L-convex function for some c ∈ R. Since the correspondence between translation-invariant L-convex functions on Zn+1 and L ♮-convex functions on Zn preserves the function values, the same statement holds for L ♮-convex functions. Consequently, we have the equivalences 

{integral translation-invariant L-convex functions on Zn+1 with an additive constant }

73 ≃ { integral L ♮-convex functions on Zn with an additive constant }≃ { integral L-convex functions of type Φ An }. (8.3) As a special case, we obtain the relations among (integral) submodular functions on {0, 1}n+1 ,(integral) spherical submodular functions of type Φ An , and (integral) submodular functions on 

V(St ΣEuc (Φ An )(0)). Here, a function ρ : {0, 1}n+1 → R ∪ { +∞} with dom ρ̸ = ∅ is said to be 

submodular if it satisfies the submodular inequality :

ρ(x) + ρ(y) ≥ ρ(x ∧ y) + ρ(x ∨ y) (x, y ∈ { 0, 1}n+1 ),

where ( x ∧ y)( i) := min {x(i), y (i)} and ( x ∨ y)( i) := max {x(i), y (i)} for i ∈ [n + 1]. A submodular function is said to be integral if it is integer-valued. Since x ∧ y = ⌊(x + y)/2⌋ and x ∨ y = ⌈(x +

y)/2⌉, we can represent the submodular inequality as: ρ(x)+ ρ(y) ≥ ρ(⌊(x+y)/2⌋)+ ρ(⌈(x+y)/2⌉)for x, y ∈ { 0, 1}n+1 . That is, submodular functions on {0, 1}n can be viewed as the restriction of L ♮-convex functions to {0, 1}n.By an argument similar to that for L- and L ♮-convex functions, we obtain 

{(integral) submodular functions ρ on {0, 1}n+1 such that ρ(0) = ρ(1n+1 ) < +∞} ≃ { (integral) submodular functions ρ of type Φ An (with ρ([0]) ∈ Z)}

and 

{(integral) submodular functions ρ on {0, 1}n+1 such that ρ(0) = ρ(1n+1 ) = 0 }≃ { (integral) spherical submodular functions of type Φ An }. (8.4) Here, the condition that ρ(0) = ρ(1n+1 ) corresponds to the translation-invariant condition for L-convex functions. 

8.1.2 M- and M ♮-convex functions 

The formal definitions of M- and M ♮-convex functions are as follows. A function ˜ g : Zn+1 →

R ∪ { +∞} with dom ˜ g̸ = ∅ is said to be M-convex if, for any p, q ∈ dom ˜ g and i ∈ [n + 1] with 

p(i) > q (i), there is j ∈ [n + 1] with p(j) < q (j) such that ˜g(p) + ˜ g(q) ≥ ˜g(p − ei + ej ) + ˜ g(q + ei − ej ). (8.5) We can easily see that the effective domain of an M-convex function ˜ g : Zn+1 → R ∪ { +∞} 

lies on the set {p ∈ Zn+1 | Pn+1  

> i=1

p(i) = r} for some r ∈ Z (see e.g., [48, Proposition 6.1]). A function on Zn+1 is said to be zero-sum if dom ˜ g ⊆ { p ∈ Zn+1 | Pn+1  

> i=1

p(i) = 0 }. Since 

L(Φ An ) = {p ∈ Zn+1 | Pn+1  

> i=1

p(i) = 0 }, a zero-sum function on Zn+1 can be naturally regarded as a function on L(Φ An ). A function g : Zn → R ∪ { +∞} with dom g̸ = ∅ is said to be M♮-convex 

if there is a zero-sum M-convex function ˜ g : Zn+1 → R ∪ { +∞} such that g(p) = ˜ g(p, − Pni=1 p(i)) for p ∈ Zn. In other words, an M ♮-convex function on Zn is the projection of a zero-sum M-convex function on Zn+1 to Zn. An M-convex or M ♮-convex function is said to be integral if it is integer-valued. The classes of zero-sum M-convex functions on Zn+1 and M-convex functions of type Φ An

are in fact naturally equivalent, after identifying the zero-sum lattice with L(Φ An ), i.e., we have 

{zero-sum M-convex functions on Zn+1 }≃ { M♮-convex functions on Zn}≃ { M-convex functions of type Φ An }.

Indeed, it is known [48, Theorem 6.43 (1)] that a function ˜ g : Zn+1 → R ∪ { +∞} with dom ˜ g̸ = ∅

is M-convex if and only if it satisfies 74 • ˜g is the restriction of conv ˜ g to Zn+1 ; and 

• Π(conv ˜ g) consists of integral M-convex polyhedra. Here, it follows from the equivalence (8.4), Corollary 5.15, and [48, (4.40) of Section 4.8], which states the equivalence between the integral M-convex polyhedra and integral submodular func-tions, that the integral M-convex polyhedra lying in {p ∈ Rn+1 | Pn+1  

> i=1

p(i) = 0 } are exactly the same as the integral M-convex polyhedra of type Φ An . Thus, by Lemma 2.5 (1), Lemma 5.20, and Proposition 5.26 (2), the second bullet is equivalent to the condition that conv ˜ g is a primal-integral locally polyhedral M-convex function. Hence, ˜ g satisfies both bullets if and only if the restriction of ˜ g to L(Φ An ) forms an M-convex function of type Φ An .In the integral case, by combining the equivalence (8.3) with the conjugacy between the integral L- and M-convex functions (Theorem 6.11 and [48, Theorem 8.12 (1)]), we immediately obtain 

{zero-sum integral M-convex functions on Zn+1 with an additive constant }≃ { integral M ♮-convex functions on Zn with an additive constant }≃ { integral M-convex functions of type Φ An }.

8.2 (Valuated) ∆-matroids and even ∆-matroids 

We compare ∆-matroids and their valuated versions with M-convex sets and functions of types Φ Bn

(or Φ Cn ) and Φ Dn . For notational simplicity, we identify a subset X ⊆ [n] with its characteristic vector eX := P 

> i∈X

ei ∈ { 0, 1}n, which induces the identification of 2 [n] with {0, 1}n. In par-ticular, we use the notation eX △ Y := eX△Y for X, Y ⊆ [n], where △ denotes the symmetric difference. For p ∈ { 0, 1}n, we refer to #supp p as the size of p.A nonempty subset B ⊆ { 0, 1}n is called a ∆ -matroid [7, 20, 14] if it satisfies the symmetric exchange axiom: for any p, q ∈ B and i ∈ supp ( p − q), there is j ∈ supp ( p − q) such that 

p △ { i, j } ∈ B. A ∆-matroid B is said to be even if the sizes of all its members have the same parity, i.e., #supp p ≡ #supp q (mod 2) for all p, q ∈ B. That is, either every member has even size or every member has odd size. For an (even) ∆-matroid B, its convex hull conv B is called the (even) ∆-matroid polytope of B.The following polyhedral characterizations are well known ([25] and [6, Section 6.3 and The-orem 6.3.1]; see also Section 8.6 below). For a nonempty subset B ⊆ { 0, 1}n, we have 

B is a ∆-matroid ⇐⇒ every edge of conv B is parallel to a root in Φ Bn , (8.6) 

B is an even ∆-matroid ⇐⇒ every edge of conv B is parallel to a root in Φ Dn , (8.7) Since a tangent cone of a polytope is generated by its incident edge directions, the above charac-terizations identify ∆-matroid polytopes and even ∆-matroid polytopes with particular M-convex polytopes of types Φ Bn and Φ Dn , respectively. Let B ⊆ { 0, 1}n be a ∆-matroid. Since L(Φ Bn ) = Zn (see Table 3), we have B ⊆ L (Φ Bn ). Then, the equivalence (8.6) immediately implies that 

{∆-matroids on [ n]} = {M-convex sets of type Φ Bn contained in {0, 1}n}. (8.8) Thus, ∆-matroids can be viewed as M-convex sets of type Φ Bn .Let B be an even ∆-matroid. Since L(Φ Dn ) = {p ∈ Zn | Pni=1 p(i) ∈ 2Z} (see Table 3), the set of p ∈ { 0, 1}n having even size equals {0, 1}n ∩ L (Φ Dn ). Thus, if every member of B has even size, then we have B ⊆ L (Φ Dn ). Then, the equivalence (8.7) immediately implies that 

{even ∆-matroids consisting of even-size elements of {0, 1}n}

75 = {M-convex sets of type Φ Dn contained in L(Φ Dn ) ∩ { 0, 1}n}. (8.9) In the case where every member of B has odd size, we consider the twist of B by an arbitrary singleton {i} ⊆ [n]. Here, for S ⊆ [n], the twist of B by S is defined by B ∗ S := {p △ S | p ∈ B},which is again a ∆-matroid (resp., an even ∆-matroid) if B is a ∆-matroid (resp., an even ∆-matroid). Since the one-element twist B ∗ { i} consists entirely of even-size feasible sets, it is an M-convex set of type Φ Dn by (8.9). Thus, even ∆-matroids can be regarded as M-convex sets of type Φ Dn , either directly or after a one-element twist. We then consider the valuated case. A function ν : {0, 1}n → R ∪ { +∞} is called a valuated 

∆-matroid (in the sense of [18]) if Π( ν) consists of ∆-matroids. Since dom ν ⊆ { 0, 1}n, which is finite, the above definition of valuated ∆-matroids immediately implies that ν = (conv ν)|{0,1}n

and Π(conv ν) = {conv B | B ∈ Π( ν)}. Combining this with the equivalence (8.8), we obtain 

{valuated ∆-matroids on {0, 1}n}≃ { M-convex functions g of type Φ Bn such that dom g ⊆ { 0, 1}n}.

Similarly, a valuated even ∆-matroid is a function ν : {0, 1}n → R ∪ { +∞} such that Π( ν)consists of even ∆-matroids; either every member of the effective domain has even size or every member of the effective domain has odd size. Then, by the same argument as above, we have 

{valuated even ∆-matroids ν such that dom ν ⊆ { 0, 1}n ∩ L (Φ Dn )}≃ { M-convex functions g of type Φ Dn such that dom g ⊆ { 0, 1}n ∩ L (Φ Dn )}.

We finally comment on the definition of valuated ∆-matroids via the simultaneous exchange property, which has been used in the field of combinatorial optimization. A ∆-matroid B is said to be symmetric (or strong ) if, for any p, q ∈ B and i ∈ supp ( p − q), there is j ∈ supp ( p − q)such that both p △ { i, j } and q △ { i, j } belong to B. It is known (see e.g., [12]) that ∆-matroids are not symmetric in general, whereas all even ∆-matroids are symmetric. In the earlier literature [21, 66, 65], valuated ∆-matroids are only defined over symmetric ∆-matroids as follows: A function ν : {0, 1}n → R ∪ { +∞} is called a valuated ∆-matroid if, for any p, q ∈ dom ν and i ∈ supp ( p − q), there is j ∈ supp ( p − q) such that 

ν(p) + ν(q) ≥ ν(p △ { i, j }) + ν(q △ { i, j }), (8.10) which is a direct generalization of the simultaneous exchange axiom of (valuated) matroids and M-convex functions (see (8.5)). The condition (8.10) immediately implies that dom ν and all members of Π( ν) form symmetric ∆-matroids. Thus, the exchange-based valuated ∆-matroids form a proper subclass of the valuated ∆-matroids in the sense of [18].

In addition, a bisubmodular polyhedron is said to be integral if it is generated from an integral normalized bisubmodular function. By using the notion of bisubmodular and M-convex polyhedra, the relations (8.13) and (8.14) can be rephrased as

We next compare BS-convex sets and functions [23, 39] with the M-convexity of type Φ Bn intro-duced in this paper. A set B ⊆ Zn is said to be BS-convex if B = B(ρ) ∩ Zn for some normalized integral bisubmodular function ρ on {− 1, 0, 1}n. Thus, it immediately follows from the identity 

L(Φ Bn ) = Zn (see Table 3) and the relation (8.15) that 

{BS-convex sets } = {M-convex sets of type Φ Bn }. (8.16) A function g : Zn → Z ∪ { +∞} (resp. g : Zn → R ∪ { +∞} ) with dom g̸ = ∅ is said to be 

BS-convex (resp. real-valued BS-convex )1 if dom g = dom (conv g) ∩ Zn, dom g = |Π( g)|, and Π( g) consists of BS-convex sets. Since a real-valued BS-convex function g is integrally convex ,i.e., (conv g)( q) = conv( g|[p,p +1n])( q) for any p ∈ Zn and q ∈ [p, p + 1n] (see [46, 55, 56]), we have g = (conv g)|Zn and dom(conv g) = S 

> B∈Π( g)

conv B. Thus, by the identity (8.16), any real-valued BS-convex function is M-convex of type Φ Bn . Since the converse clearly holds, we obtain 

{BS-convex functions } = {integer-valued M-convex functions of type Φ Bn }

⊊ {integral M-convex functions of type Φ Bn }

⊊ {M-convex functions of type Φ Bn }

= {real-valued BS-convex functions }.

8.5 UJ-convex functions 

A function f : Zn → R ∪ { +∞} with dom f̸ = ∅ is said to be UJ-convex [23] (see also [31, Example 12]) if its Lov´ asz extension f along with the Euclidean Coxeter complex Σ Euc (Φ Cn )forms a convex function. By the above definition, the identity V(Σ Euc (Φ Cn )) = Zn (see Table 5), and Theorem 7.2, UJ-convex functions and L-convex functions of type Φ Cn are exactly the same objects, i.e., 

{UJ-convex functions on Zn} = {L-convex functions of type Φ Cn }.

A UJ-convex function is said to be integral if it is integer-valued. On the other hand, by Theorem 6.4, an L-convex function f : Zn → R ∪ { +∞} of type Φ Cn is integral (in our sense) if 

f (x) ∈

(

Z ∪ { +∞} if x ∈ (2 Z)n ∪ (2 Z + 1) n,

Z/2 ∪ { +∞} otherwise; see Table 5 for the mark function of Σ Euc (Φ Cn ). Hence, every integral UJ-convex function is an integral L-convex function of type Φ Cn , but the converse need not hold. Consequently, for n ≥ 2, we obtain 

{integral UJ-convex functions on Zn} ⊊ {integral L-convex functions of type Φ Cn }.          

> 1In [23], the definition requires only that every member of Π( g) be a BS-convex set. This condition alone does not imply integral convexity. The strengthened definition adopted here, which additionally requires dom g=dom(conv g)∩Znand dom g=|Π( g)|, was formulated in consultation with Satoru Fujishige, who is the author of [23], through private communication.

79 8.6 Coxeter matroids 

Let Φ be an irreducible root system of rank n, and let WΦ be its Weyl group (recall the defi-nition (3.3) of WΦ). Fix a point p ∈ V ∗ and consider its Weyl-group orbit WΦp. A nonempty subset B ⊆ WΦp is a Coxeter matroid [6] if B satisfies the maximality property (MP), i.e., for every simple system ∆ of Φ, the set B has a unique maximal member with respect to ≤∆.Since all points in WΦp have the same norm by WΦ ≤ O( V ∗), every member of B is a vertex of conv B. The Coxeter-matroid maximality property is therefore equivalent to the condition that, for every simple system ∆, the polytope conv B has a unique maximal member with respect to ≤∆. By Corollary 5.23, this is equivalent to the M-convexity of conv B. Since the tangent cone of a polytope at a vertex is generated by the directions of the incident edges, a polytope is M-convex of type Φ if and only if every edge is parallel to a root in Φ. Consequently, for 

B ⊆ WΦp, we have 

B is a Coxeter matroid ⇐⇒ conv B is an M-convex polytope of type Φ (8.17) 

⇐⇒ every edge of conv B is parallel to a root in Φ .

This recovers the polyhedral characterization of Coxeter matroids due to Gelfand and Serganova [26], usually referred to as the Gelfand–Serganova theorem ; see [6, Section 6.3 and Theorem 6.3.1]. In type Φ An , after translating an ordinary matroid base polytope to the zero-sum hyperplane, this characterization specializes to the classical edge characterization of matroid base polytopes due to Gelfand, Goresky, MacPherson, and Serganova [25, Theorem 4.1] (see also [22, Theorem 17.1]). As we can see above (8.17), Coxeter matroid polytopes , which are the convex hulls of Coxeter matroids, are precisely the M-convex polytopes whose vertex sets are contained in a single Weyl-group orbit. In addition, conv B is integral in the sense of this paper if and only if p ∈ L (Φ). Indeed, if p ∈ L (Φ), then B ⊆ L (Φ) and conv B is integral. Conversely, if conv B is integral, then each of its vertices belongs to L(Φ); hence one, and therefore every, point in WΦp belongs to L(Φ).
